# Afghanistan

This article is about the country. For other uses, see [Afghanistan (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghanistan_\(disambiguation\)).

"Islamic Emirate of Afghanistan" redirects here. For the group, see [Taliban](30635.md). For the state that existed from 1996 to 2001, see [Islamic Emirate of Afghanistan (1996–2001)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Emirate_of_Afghanistan_\(1996%E2%80%932001\)).

**Afghanistan**,[^cite_note-25] officially the **Islamic Emirate of Afghanistan**,[^cite_note-26] is a [landlocked country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landlocked_country) located at the crossroads of [Central](6742.md) and [South Asia](21566765.md). It is bordered by [Pakistan](23235.md) to the [east and south](203570.md),[^cite_note-28] [Iran](14653.md) to the [west](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghanistan%E2%80%93Iran_border), [Turkmenistan](198149.md) to the [northwest](58436304.md), [Uzbekistan](31853.md) to the [north](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghanistan%E2%80%93Uzbekistan_border), [Tajikistan](30108.md) to the [northeast](58437163.md), and [China](5405.md) to the [northeast and east](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghanistan%E2%80%93China_border). Occupying 652,864 square kilometers (252,072 sq mi) of land, the country is predominantly mountainous with plains [in the north](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_Turkestan) and [the southwest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sistan_Basin), which are separated by the [Hindu Kush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindu_Kush) mountain range. [Kabul](16826.md) is the country's capital and largest city. [Afghanistan's population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Afghanistan) is estimated to be between 35 and 50 million.[^cite_note-pop_note-21]

[Human habitation in Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_history_of_Afghanistan) dates to the [Middle Paleolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Paleolithic) era. The country's land [has witnessed numerous military campaigns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Invasions_of_Afghanistan), including those by the [Achaemenids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Achaemenid_Empire), [Alexander the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_the_Great), the [Maurya Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maurya_Empire), [Arab Muslims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_conquests_of_Afghanistan), the [Mongols](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongol_invasion_of_the_Khwarazmian_Empire), the [British](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_influence_in_Afghanistan#The_Great_Game), the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet%E2%80%93Afghan_War), and a [United States-led coalition](19666611.md). Afghanistan also served as the source from which the [Greco-Bactrians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greco-Bactrian_Kingdom) and the [Mughals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mughal_Empire), among others, rose to form major empires.[^cite_note-37] Because of the various conquests and periods in both the [Iranian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_Iran) and [Indian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_India) cultural spheres,[^cite_note-38][^cite_note-39] the area was a center for [Zoroastrianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zoroastrianism), [Buddhism](3267529.md), [Hinduism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism), and later [Islam](6037917.md).[^cite_note-40] The modern state of Afghanistan began with the [Durrani Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Durrani_Empire) in the 18th century under [Ahmad Shah Durrani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmad_Shah_Durrani),[^cite_note-41] although [Dost Mohammad Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dost_Mohammad_Khan) is sometimes considered to be the founder of the first [modern Afghan state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emirate_of_Afghanistan).[^cite_note-42] Afghanistan became a [buffer state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buffer_state) in the [Great Game](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Game) between the [British Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Empire) and the [Russian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Empire). From [India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Raj), the British attempted to subjugate Afghanistan but were repelled in the [First Anglo-Afghan War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Anglo-Afghan_War); the [Second Anglo-Afghan War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Anglo-Afghan_War) saw a British victory. Following the [Third Anglo-Afghan War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_Anglo-Afghan_War) in 1919, Afghanistan became free of foreign political hegemony, and emerged as the independent [Kingdom of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Afghanistan) in 1926; this monarchy lasted almost half a century.&#x20;

Since the late 1970s, Afghanistan's history has been dominated by extensive warfare, including [coups, invasions, insurgencies, and civil wars](27576770.md). This period of instability began in 1973 when King [Zahir Shah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammed_Zahir_Shah) was [overthrown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1973_Afghan_coup_d'%C3%A9tat) by his cousin [Mohammad Daoud Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad_Daoud_Khan), ending the monarchy and resulting in the establishment of the [Republic of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_Afghanistan_\(1973%E2%80%931978\)). Daoud Khan became the [President of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_Afghanistan), ruling as an [autocrat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autocracy). In 1978 the conflict became a violent one when [a communist revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saur_Revolution) overthrew and assassinated Daoud Khan, establishing the socialist [Democratic Republic of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Republic_of_Afghanistan). Subsequent infighting prompted the [Soviet Union to invade Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_invasion_of_Afghanistan) in 1979.&#x20;

The [*mujahideen*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_mujahideen) won against the Soviets in the [Soviet–Afghan War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet%E2%80%93Afghan_War), leading to the [Soviets' withdrawal in 1989](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_withdrawal_from_Afghanistan). The ensuing [civil war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_Civil_War_\(1989%E2%80%931992\)) overthrew the socialist republic and established the [Islamic State of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_State_of_Afghanistan) through the [Peshawar Accord](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peshawar_Accord), which could not remain in force and resulted in a [second civil war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_Civil_War_\(1992%E2%80%931996\)) between *mujahideen* factions in 1992. The [Taliban](30635.md) controlled most of the country by 1996, but their [Islamic Emirate of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Emirate_of_Afghanistan_\(1996%E2%80%932001\)) received little international recognition before its overthrow in the 2001 [US invasion of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_invasion_of_Afghanistan). The Taliban returned to power in 2021 after [capturing Kabul](68481047.md), ending the [2001–2021 war](19666611.md). The Taliban government is [widely unrecognized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recognition_of_the_Islamic_Emirate_of_Afghanistan)[^cite_note-44] by the international community due to violations of [human rights in Afghanistan](4317321.md), particularly regarding the [treatment of women by the Taliban](31285.md).

Afghanistan is rich in natural resources, including [lithium](17561.md), iron, [zinc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zinc), and copper. It is the third largest producer of both [saffron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saffron)[^cite_note-45] and [cashmere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cashmere_wool).[^cite_note-46] The country is a member of the [South Asian Association for Regional Cooperation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Asian_Association_for_Regional_Cooperation) and a founding member of the [Organization of Islamic Cooperation](47725.md). Due to the effects of war in recent decades, the country has dealt with high levels of terrorism, [poverty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poverty_in_Afghanistan), and child malnutrition. Afghanistan remains among the world's [least developed countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Least_developed_country) with [its economic output](9895.md) per capita among the [lowest of any country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_\(nominal\)_per_capita).

## Etymology

Main article: [Name of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Name_of_Afghanistan)

The precise origin of the name is uncertain and heavily debated. Some scholars suggest that the [root](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Root_\(linguistics\)) name [*Afghān*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_\(ethnonym\)) is derived from the [Sanskrit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanskrit) word *[Aśvakan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A%C5%9Bvaka)*, which was the name used for ancient inhabitants of the [Hindu Kush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindu_Kush).[^cite_note-47] *Aśvakan* literally means "horsemen", "horse breeders", or "[cavalrymen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cavalry)" (from *[aśva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A%C5%9Bva)*, the Sanskrit and [Avestan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avestan) words for "horse").[^cite_note-48]

Historically, the ethnonym *Afghān* was used to refer to ethnic [Pashtuns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pashtuns) in the [Persian language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_language).[^cite_note-49] The Arabic and Persian form of the name, *Afġān*, was first attested in the 10th-century geography book *[Hudud al-'Alam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hudud_al-'Alam)*.[^cite_note-50] The last part of the name, "*[-stan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/-stan)*", is a Persian suffix meaning "place of". Therefore, "Afghanistan" translates to "land of the Afghans", or "land of the Pashtuns" in a historical sense. According to the third edition of the *[Encyclopaedia of Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Encyclopaedia_of_Islam)*:[^cite_note-51]

> The name Afghanistan (Afghānistān, land of the Afghans / Pashtuns, *afāghina*, sing. *afghān*) can be traced to the early eighth/fourteenth century, when it designated the easternmost part of the [Kartid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kartid) realm. This name was later used for certain regions in the [Ṣafavid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Safavid_Iran) and [Mughal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mughal_Empire) empires that were inhabited by Afghans. While based on a state-supporting elite of [Abdālī / Durrānī](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Durrani) Afghans, the [Sadūzāʾī Durrānī polity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Durrani_Empire) that came into being in 1160 / 1747 was not called Afghanistan in its own day. The name became a state designation only during the colonial intervention of the nineteenth century.

The term "Afghanistan" was officially used in 1855, when the British recognized [Dost Mohammad Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dost_Mohammad_Khan) as king of [Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emirate_of_Afghanistan).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELee2019317-52]

## History

Main article: [History of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Afghanistan)

### Prehistory and antiquity

Main article: [Ancient history of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_history_of_Afghanistan)

See also: [Hindu and Buddhist heritage of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindu_and_Buddhist_heritage_of_Afghanistan)



Tents of Afghan [nomads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nomad) in the northern [Badghis Province](23537888.md). Early peasant farming villages came into existence about 7,000 years ago.

Excavations of prehistoric sites suggest that humans were living in what is now Afghanistan at least 50,000 years ago, and that farming communities in the area were among the earliest in the world. An important site of early historical activities, many believe that Afghanistan compares to [Egypt](8087628.md) in the historical value of its archaeological sites.[^cite_note-JFS-53][^cite_note-54] Artifacts typical of the [Paleolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paleolithic), [Mesolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mesolithic), [Neolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neolithic), [Bronze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bronze_Age), and [Iron Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron_Age) have been found in Afghanistan. Urban civilization is believed to have begun as early as 3000 BCE, and the early city of [Mundigak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mundigak) (near [Kandahar](17260.md) in the south of the country) was a center of the [Helmand culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helmand_culture). More recent findings established that the [Indus Valley Civilization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indus_Valley_Civilization) stretched up towards modern-day Afghanistan. An Indus Valley site has been found on the [Oxus River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oxus_River) at [Shortugai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shortugai) in northern Afghanistan.[^cite_note-55][^cite_note-56][^cite_note-57]

After 2000 BCE successive waves of semi-nomadic people from Central Asia began moving south into Afghanistan; among them were many [Indo-European](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-European_languages)-speaking [Indo-Iranians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Iranians). These tribes later migrated further into South Asia, Western Asia, and toward Europe via the area north of the [Caspian Sea](19653787.md). The region at the time was referred to as [Ariana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ariana).[^cite_note-JFS-53][^cite_note-58] By the middle of the 6th century BCE, the Achaemenids overthrew the [Medes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medes) and incorporated [Arachosia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arachosia), [Aria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aria_\(satrapy\)), and [Bactria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bactria) within its eastern boundaries. An [inscription](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigraphy) on the tombstone of [Darius I of Persia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Darius_I_of_Persia) mentions the [Kabul Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kabulistan) in a list of the 29 countries that he had conquered.[^cite_note-59] The region of [Arachosia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arachosia), around Kandahar in modern-day southern Afghanistan, used to be primarily Zoroastrian and played a key role in the transfer of the Avesta to [Persia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persis) and is thus considered by some to be the "second homeland of Zoroastrianism".[^cite_note-Gnoli-1989-60][^cite_note-Gnoli-1989a-61][^cite_note-ARACHOSIA_–_Encyclopaedia_Iranica-62]



A "Bactrian gold" [Scythian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scythian) belt depicting [Dionysus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dionysus), from [Tillya Tepe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tillya_Tepe) in the ancient region of [Bactria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bactria)

[Alexander the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_the_Great) and his Macedonian forces arrived in Afghanistan in 330 BCE after defeating [Darius III of Persia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Darius_III_of_Persia) a year earlier in the [Battle of Gaugamela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Gaugamela). Following Alexander's brief occupation, the successor state of the [Seleucid Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seleucid_Empire) controlled the region until 305 BCE, when they gave much of it to the [Maurya Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maurya_Empire) as part of an alliance treaty. The Mauryans controlled the area south of the [Hindu Kush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindu_Kush) until they were overthrown in about 185 BCE. Their decline began 60 years after [Ashoka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashoka)'s rule ended, leading to the [Hellenistic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hellenistic) reconquest by the [Greco-Bactrians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greco-Bactrians). Much of it soon broke away and became part of the [Indo-Greek Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Greek_Kingdom). They were defeated and expelled by the [Indo-Scythians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Scythians) in the late 2nd century BCE.[^cite_note-63][^cite_note-FOOTNOTERunion200744-64] The [Silk Road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silk_Road) appeared during the first century BCE, and Afghanistan flourished with trade, with routes to China, India, Persia, and north to the cities of [Bukhara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bukhara), [Samarkand](51491.md), and [Khiva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khiva) in present-day Uzbekistan.[^cite_note-65] Goods and ideas were exchanged at this center point, such as Chinese silk, Persian silver and Roman gold, while the region of present Afghanistan was mining and trading [lapis lazuli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lapis_lazuli) stones[^cite_note-66] mainly from the [Badakhshan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Badakhshan) region.

During the first century BCE, the [Parthian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parthian_Empire) subjugated the region but lost it to their [Indo-Parthian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Parthian) vassals. In the mid-to-late first century CE the vast [Kushan Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kushan_Empire), centered in Afghanistan, became great patrons of Buddhist culture, making [Buddhism](3267529.md) flourish throughout the region. The Kushans were overthrown by the [Sassanids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sassanids) in the 3rd century CE, though the [Indo-Sassanids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Sassanids) continued to rule at least parts of the region. They were followed by the [Kidarites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kidarite) who, in turn, was replaced by the [Hephthalites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hephthalites). They were replaced by the [Turk Shahi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turk_Shahi) in the 7th century. The Buddhist Turk Shahi of Kabul was replaced by a Hindu dynasty before the Saffarids conquered the area in 870; this Hindu dynasty was called [Hindu Shahi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindu_Shahi).[^cite_note-67] Much of the northeastern and southern areas of the country remained dominated by [Buddhist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist) culture.[^cite_note-68][^cite_note-69]

### Medieval period

Main articles: [Muslim conquests of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_conquests_of_Afghanistan), [Mongol campaigns in Central Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongol_campaigns_in_Central_Asia), and [Ghaznavid campaigns in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghaznavid_campaigns_in_India)



The [Ghurids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghurid_dynasty) originated from [Ghor Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghor_Province) in central Afghanistan.

[Arab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab) [Muslims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim) brought Islam to [Herat](14128.md) and [Zaranj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zaranj) in 642 CE and began spreading eastward; some of the native inhabitants they encountered accepted it while others revolted. Before the [arrival of Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_conquest_of_Afghanistan), the region used to be home to various beliefs and cults, often resulting in [Syncretism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syncretism) between the dominant religions[^cite_note-70][^cite_note-71] such as [Zoroastrianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zoroastrianism),[^cite_note-Gnoli-1989-60][^cite_note-Gnoli-1989a-61][^cite_note-ARACHOSIA_–_Encyclopaedia_Iranica-62] Buddhism or [Greco-Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greco-Buddhism), [Ancient Iranian religions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Iranian_religion),[^cite_note-Allen-72] [Hinduism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism), Christianity,[^cite_note-73][^cite_note-74] and Judaism.[^cite_note-75][^cite_note-76] An exemplification of the syncretism in the region would be that people were patrons of Buddhism but still worshipped local Iranian gods such as [Ahura Mazda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahura_Mazda), [Lady Nana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nana_\(Kushan_goddess\)), [Anahita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anahita) or [Mihr (Mithra)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mithra) and portrayed [Greek gods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_mythology) as protectors of Buddha.[^cite_note-77][^cite_note-Allen-72][^cite_note-78] The [Zunbils](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zunbils) and Kabul Shahi were first conquered in 870 CE by the [Saffarid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saffarid_dynasty) Muslims of Zaranj. Later, the [Samanids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samanids) extended their Islamic influence south of the Hindu Kush. The [Ghaznavids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghaznavids) rose to power in the 10th century.[^cite_note-79][^cite_note-80][^cite_note-81]

By the 11th century, [Mahmud of Ghazni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahmud_of_Ghazni) had defeated the remaining Hindu rulers and effectively [Islamized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamized) the wider region,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEwans200222–23-82] with the exception of [Kafiristan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kafiristan).[^cite_note-83] Mahmud made [Ghazni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghazni) into an important city and patronized intellectuals such as the historian [al-Biruni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Biruni) and the poet [Ferdowsi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ferdowsi).[^cite_note-84] The [Ghaznavid dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghaznavid_dynasty) was [overthrown by the Ghurids in 1186](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Lahore_\(1186\)), whose architectural achievements included the remote [Minaret of Jam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minaret_of_Jam). The Ghurids controlled Afghanistan for less than a century before being conquered by the [Khwarazmian dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khwarazmian_dynasty) in 1215.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEwans200223-85]



[Mongol invasions and conquests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongol_invasions_and_conquests)

In 1219 CE, [Genghis Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genghis_Khan) and his [Mongol army overran the region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongol_invasion_of_the_Khwarazmian_Empire). His troops are said to have annihilated the Khwarazmian cities of Herat and [Balkh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balkh) as well as [Bamyan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Bamyan).[^cite_note-86] The destruction caused by the Mongols forced many locals to return to an agrarian rural society.[^cite_note-87] Mongol rule continued with the [Ilkhanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ilkhanate) in the northwest while the [Khalji dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khalji_dynasty) administered the Afghan tribal areas south of the Hindu Kush until the invasion of [Timur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timur) (aka Tamerlane), who established the [Timurid Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timurid_Empire) in 1370. Under the rule of [Shah Rukh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shah_Rukh), the city of Herat[^cite_note-88] served as the focal point of the [Timurid Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timurid_Renaissance), whose glory matched [Florence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florence) of the [Italian Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_Renaissance) as the center of a cultural rebirth.[^cite_note-89][^cite_note-90]

In the early 16th century [Babur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Babur) arrived from [Ferghana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ferghana) and captured Kabul from the [Arghun dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arghun_dynasty).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarfield201292–93-91] Babur would go on to conquer the Afghan [Lodi dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lodi_dynasty) who had ruled the Delhi Sultanate in the [First Battle of Panipat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Battle_of_Panipat).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarfield201275-92] Between the 16th and 18th century, the Uzbek [Khanate of Bukhara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khanate_of_Bukhara), Iranian [Safavids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Safavids), and Indian [Mughals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mughals) ruled parts of the territory.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDupree1997319,_321-93] During the medieval period, the northwestern area of Afghanistan was referred to by the regional name [Khorasan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_Khorasan), which was commonly used up to the 19th century among natives to describe their country.[^cite_note-94][^cite_note-95][^cite_note-96][^cite_note-97]

### Hotak dynasty

Main article: [Hotak dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hotak_dynasty)



Map of the Hotak Empire at its height in 1728, disputed between [Hussain Hotak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hussain_Hotak) (centered in Kandahar) and [Ashraf Hotak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashraf_Hotak) (centered in Isfahan)

In 1709, [Mirwais Hotak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mirwais_Hotak), a local [Ghilzai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghilzai) tribal leader, successfully rebelled against the Safavids. He defeated [George XI (Gurgin Khan)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_XI), the Georgian governor of Kandahar under the Safavids, and established his own kingdom.[^cite_note-Browne-98] Mirwais died in 1715, and was succeeded by his brother [Abdul Aziz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdul_Aziz_Hotak), who was soon killed by Mirwais's son [Mahmud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahmud_Hotak) for possibly planning to sign a peace with the Safavids. Mahmud led the Afghan army in 1722 to the Persian capital of [Isfahan](223149.md), and captured the city after the [Battle of Gulnabad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Gulnabad) and proclaimed himself King of Persia.[^cite_note-Browne-98] The Afghan dynasty was ousted from Persia by [Nader Shah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nader_Shah) after the 1729 [Battle of Damghan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Damghan_\(1729\)).

In 1738, Nader Shah and his [forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afsharid_dynasty) captured Kandahar in the [siege of Kandahar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Kandahar), the last Hotak stronghold, from Shah [Hussain Hotak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hussain_Hotak). Soon after, the Persian and Afghan forces [invaded India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nader_Shah's_invasion_of_India), Nader Shah had plundered Delhi, alongside his 16-year-old commander, [Ahmad Shah Durrani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmad_Shah_Durrani) who had assisted him on these campaigns. Nader Shah was assassinated in 1747.[^cite_note-99][^cite_note-100]

### Durrani Empire

Main article: [Durrani Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Durrani_Empire)

See also: [Ahmad Shah Durrani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmad_Shah_Durrani)

Ahmad Shah had launched two campaigns into Khorasan, 1750–1751 and 1754–1755.[^cite_note-101]



Portrait of [Ahmad Shah Durrani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmad_Shah_Durrani), c. 1757

### Barakzai dynasty and British wars

Further information: [Dost Mohammad Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dost_Mohammad_Khan), [Herat (1793–1863)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herat_\(1793%E2%80%931863\)), [Maimana Khanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maimana_Khanate), [Principality of Kandahar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Principality_of_Kandahar), [European influence in Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_influence_in_Afghanistan), [Anglo-Afghan Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-Afghan_Wars), [Durand Line](203570.md), and [Afghan Civil War (1928–1929)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_Civil_War_\(1928%E2%80%931929\))



Map of Afghanistan ([Emirate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emirate_of_Afghanistan)) and surrounding nations in 1839, during the [First Anglo-Afghan War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Anglo-Afghan_War). [Dost Mohammad Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dost_Mohammad_Khan)'s realm can be seen as the Emirate of Kabul, with the [Principality of Qandahar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Principality_of_Qandahar) and the [Emirate of Herat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emirate_of_Herat) seen as well.

By the early 19th century, the Afghan empire was under threat from the [Persians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qajar_dynasty) in the west and the [Sikh Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikh_Empire) in the east. [Fateh Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fateh_Khan_Barakzai), leader of the [Barakzai tribe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barakzai_dynasty), installed many of his brothers in positions of power throughout the empire. Fateh Khan was [brutally murdered](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torture_and_Execution_of_Fateh_Khan_Barakzai) in 1818 by [Mahmud Shah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahmud_Shah_Durrani). As a result, the brothers of Fateh Khan and the [Barakzai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barakzai) tribe rebelled, and a civil war brewed. During this turbulent period, Afghanistan fractured into many states, including the [Principality of Qandahar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Principality_of_Qandahar), [Emirate of Herat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herat_\(1793%E2%80%931863\)), [Khanate of Qunduz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kunduz_Khanate), [Maimana Khanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maimana_Khanate), and numerous other warring polities. The most prominent state was the [Emirate of Kabul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emirate_of_Afghanistan), ruled by [Dost Mohammad Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dost_Mohammad_Khan).[^cite_note-102][^cite_note-103]

[Akbar Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wazir_Akbar_Khan) and the Afghan army failed to capture the [Jamrud Fort](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jamrud_Fort) from the [Sikh Khalsa Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikh_Khalsa_Army), but killed Sikh commander [Hari Singh Nalwa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hari_Singh_Nalwa), thus ending the [Afghan-Sikh Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan-Sikh_Wars).[^cite_note-104]



[Afghan tribesmen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pashtun_tribes) in 1841, painted by British officer [James Rattray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Rattray)

&#x20;In 1893, Abdur Rahman signed an agreement in which the ethnic Pashtun territories were divided by the [Durand Line](203570.md), which forms the modern-day border between Pakistan and Afghanistan. [Hazara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hazaras) dominated [Hazaristan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hazarajat)[^cite_note-105] and pagan [Kafiristan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kafiristan) remained politically independent until being [conquered](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_conquests_of_Afghanistan) by Abdur Rahman Khan in 1891–1896. He was known as the "Iron Amir" for his features and his ruthless methods against tribes.[^cite_note-106] He died in 1901, succeeded by his son, [Habibullah Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Habibullah_Khan).

> How can a small power like Afghanistan, which is like a goat between these lions \[Britain and Russia] or a grain of wheat between two strong millstones of the grinding mill, stand in the midway of the stones without being ground to dust?

— [Abdur Rahman Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdur_Rahman_Khan), the "Iron Amir", in 1900[^cite_note-107][^cite_note-108]

During the [First World War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_World_War), when Afghanistan was neutral, Habibullah Khan was met by officials of the [Central Powers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Powers) in the [Niedermayer–Hentig Expedition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Niedermayer%E2%80%93Hentig_Expedition). They called on Afghanistan to declare full independence from the United Kingdom, join them and attack British India, as part of the [Hindu–German Conspiracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindu%E2%80%93German_Conspiracy). The effort to bring Afghanistan into the Central Powers failed, but it sparked discontent among the population about maintaining neutrality with the British. Habibullah was assassinated in February 1919, and [Amanullah Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amanullah_Khan) eventually assumed power. A staunch supporter of the 1915–1916 expeditions, Amanullah Khan invaded British India, beginning the [Third Anglo-Afghan War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_Anglo-Afghan_War), and entering British India via the [Khyber Pass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khyber_Pass).[^cite_note-109]



[Amanullah Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amanullah_Khan) proclaimed himself [King of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_of_Afghanistan) in June 1926.

Following the end of the Third Anglo-Afghan War and the signing of the [Treaty of Rawalpindi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-Afghan_Treaty_of_1919) on 19 August 1919, [Emir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emir_of_Afghanistan) Amanullah Khan declared the [Emirate of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emirate_of_Afghanistan) a [sovereign](23604120.md) and fully [independent state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independent_state). He moved to end his country's traditional isolation by establishing diplomatic relations with the international community, particularly with the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union) and the [Weimar Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weimar_Republic).[^cite_note-110][^cite_note-111] He proclaimed himself [King of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_of_Afghanistan) on 9 June 1926, forming the [Kingdom of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Afghanistan). He introduced several reforms intended to modernize his nation. A key force behind these reforms was [Mahmud Tarzi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahmud_Tarzi), an ardent supporter of the education of women. He fought for Article 68 of Afghanistan's [1923 constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1923_Constitution_of_Afghanistan), which implemented compulsory elementary education. [Slavery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery) was abolished in 1923.[^cite_note-112] King Amanullah's wife, [Queen Soraya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soraya_Tarzi), was an important figure during this period in the fight for women's education and against their oppression.[^cite_note-113]

Some of the reforms, such as the abolition of the traditional [burqa](165826.md) for women and the opening of co-educational schools, alienated many tribal and religious leaders, leading to the [Afghan Civil War (1928–1929)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_Civil_War_\(1928%E2%80%931929\)). King Amanullah abdicated in January 1929, and soon after Kabul fell to [Saqqawist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saqqawists) forces led by [Habibullah Kalakani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Habibullah_Kalakani).[^cite_note-114] [Mohammad Nadir Shah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad_Nadir_Shah), Amanullah's cousin, defeated and killed Kalakani in October 1929, and was declared King Nadir Shah.[^cite_note-115] He abandoned the reforms of King Amanullah in favor of a more gradual approach to modernization, but was assassinated in 1933 by [Abdul Khaliq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdul_Khaliq_Hazara_\(assassin\)).[^cite_note-116]

Mohammed Zahir Shah succeeded to the throne and reigned as king from 1933 to 1973. During the [tribal revolts of 1944–1947](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_tribal_revolts_of_1944%E2%80%931947), King Zahir's reign was challenged by [Zadran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zadran_\(Pashtun_tribe\)), [Safi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Safi_\(tribe\)), [Mangal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mangal_\(Pashtun_tribe\)), and [Wazir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wazir_\(Pashtun_tribe\)) tribesmen led by [Mazrak Zadran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mazrak_Zadran), [Salemai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salemai), and [Faqir of Ipi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Faqir_of_Ipi), among others – many of whom were [Amanullah loyalists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amanullah_loyalism). Afghanistan joined the [League of Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/League_of_Nations) in 1934. In the 1930s, the country experienced significant development of roads, infrastructure, the founding of a [national bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Da_Afghanistan_Bank), and increased education. Road links in the north played a large part in a growing cotton and textile industry.[^cite_note-Eur2002-117] The country built close relationships with the [Axis powers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Axis_powers), with [Nazi Germany](21212.md) having the largest share in Afghan development at the time.[^cite_note-118]



[King Zahir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammed_Zahir_Shah), the last reigning monarch of Afghanistan, who reigned from 1933 until 1973

Until 1946, King Zahir ruled with the assistance of his uncle, who held the post of [prime minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_Afghanistan) and continued the policies of Nadir Shah. Another uncle, [Shah Mahmud Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shah_Mahmud_Khan), became prime minister in 1946 and experimented with allowing greater political freedom. He was replaced in 1953 by [Mohammed Daoud Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammed_Daoud_Khan), a staunch Pashtun nationalist who sought to incorporate [Pashtun regions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pashtunistan) of Pakistan into Afghanistan, leading to highly tense relations between the two countries.[^cite_note-119] In 1960, Khan directed Afghan armed forces to [invade Bajaur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bajaur_campaign) and capture the town along with other Pashtun areas but they were defeated by Pakistani forces.[^cite_note-Shaista_Wahab-120][^cite_note-FOOTNOTERiedel2014-121] Daoud Khan pressed for social modernization reforms and sought a closer relationship with the Soviet Union. Afterward, the [1964 constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1964_Constitution_of_Afghanistan) was formed, and the first non-royal prime minister was sworn in.[^cite_note-Eur2002-117]

Zahir Shah, like his father Nadir Shah, had a policy of maintaining national independence while pursuing gradual modernization, creating nationalist feeling, and improving relations with the United Kingdom. Afghanistan was neither a participant in [World War II](32927.md) nor aligned with either power bloc in the [Cold War](325329.md). However, it was a beneficiary of the latter rivalry as both the Soviet Union and the United States vied for influence by building Afghanistan's main highways, airports, and other vital infrastructure. On a per capita basis, Afghanistan received more Soviet [development aid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Development_aid) than any other country. In 1973, while the King was in Italy, Daoud Khan launched a [bloodless coup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1973_Afghan_coup) and became the first [president of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_Afghanistan), abolishing the monarchy.

### Democratic Republic and Soviet War

Main articles: [Democratic Republic of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Republic_of_Afghanistan), [Soviet–Afghan War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet%E2%80%93Afghan_War), and [Afghan Civil War (1989–1992)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_Civil_War_\(1989%E2%80%931992\))

Further information: [History of Afghanistan (1978–1992)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Afghanistan_\(1978%E2%80%931992\))



Soviet troops in [Gardez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gardez), Afghanistan, in 1987

In April 1978, the communist [People's Democratic Party of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Democratic_Party_of_Afghanistan) (PDPA) seized power in a bloody coup d'état against then-President [Mohammed Daoud Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammed_Daoud_Khan), in what is called the [Saur Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saur_Revolution). The PDPA declared the establishment of the [Democratic Republic of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Republic_of_Afghanistan), with its first leader named as [People's Democratic Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Democratic_Party_of_Afghanistan) General Secretary [Nur Muhammad Taraki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nur_Muhammad_Taraki).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEwans2002186–88-122] This would trigger a series of events that would dramatically turn Afghanistan from a peaceful (albeit poor and secluded) country to a hotbed of conflict and terrorism.[^cite_note-123] The PDPA initiated various social, symbolic, and land distribution reforms that provoked strong opposition, while also brutally oppressing political dissidents. This caused unrest and quickly expanded into a state of [civil war](27576770.md) by 1979, waged by guerrilla *[mujahideen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mujahideen)* (and smaller [Maoist](46636.md) guerrillas) against regime forces countrywide. It quickly turned into a [proxy war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proxy_war) as the Pakistani government provided these rebels with covert training centers, the United States [supported](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Cyclone) them through Pakistan's [Inter-Services Intelligence](2159021.md) (ISI),[^cite_note-124] and the Soviet Union sent thousands of military advisers to support the PDPA regime.[^cite_note-125] Meanwhile, there was increasingly hostile friction between the competing factions of the PDPA – the dominant [Khalq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khalq) and the more moderate [Parcham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parcham).[^cite_note-126]

In October 1979, PDPA General Secretary Taraki was assassinated in an internal coup orchestrated by then-prime minister [Hafizullah Amin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hafizullah_Amin), who became the new general secretary of the PDPA. The situation in the country deteriorated under Amin, and thousands of people went missing.[^cite_note-127] Displeased with Amin's government, the [Soviet Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Armed_Forces) invaded the country in December 1979, heading for Kabul and killing Amin.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarfield2012234-128] A Soviet-organized regime, led by Parcham's [Babrak Karmal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Babrak_Karmal) but inclusive of both factions (Parcham and Khalq), filled the vacuum. Soviet troops in more substantial numbers were deployed to stabilize Afghanistan under Karmal, marking the beginning of the [Soviet–Afghan War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet%E2%80%93Afghan_War).[^cite_note-129] Lasting nine years, the war caused the deaths of between 562,000[^cite_note-130] and 2 million Afghans,[^cite_note-138] and displaced about 6 million people who subsequently fled Afghanistan, mainly to Pakistan and Iran.[^cite_note-139] Heavy air bombardment destroyed many countryside villages, millions of [landmines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landmine) were planted,[^cite_note-140] and some cities such as Herat and Kandahar were also damaged from bombardment. After the [Soviet withdrawal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_withdrawal_from_Afghanistan), another [civil war ensued](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_Civil_War_\(1989-92\)) until the communist regime under PDPA leader [Mohammad Najibullah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad_Najibullah) collapsed in 1992.[^cite_note-141][^cite_note-142][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarfield2012239,_244-143]

The Soviet–Afghan War had drastic social effects on Afghanistan. The militarization of society led to heavily armed police, private bodyguards, openly armed civil defense groups, and the like becoming the norm in Afghanistan for decades thereafter.[^cite_note-144] The traditional power structure had shifted from the clergy, community elders, intelligentsia, and military in favor of powerful [warlords](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warlords).[^cite_note-145]

### Post–Cold War conflict

See also: [Afghan Civil War (1992–1996)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_Civil_War_\(1992%E2%80%931996\)), [Afghan Civil War (1996–2001)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_Civil_War_\(1996%E2%80%932001\)), [Islamic Emirate of Afghanistan (1996–2001)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Emirate_of_Afghanistan_\(1996%E2%80%932001\)), and [Northern Alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Alliance)



Development of the civil war from 1992 to late 2001

Another civil war broke out after the [creation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peshawar_Accords) of a dysfunctional coalition [government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_State_of_Afghanistan) between leaders of various *mujahideen* factions. Amid a state of anarchy and factional infighting,[^cite_note-146][^cite_note-Human_Rights_Watch-2005-147][^cite_note-148] various *mujahideen* factions committed widespread rape, murder, and extortion,[^cite_note-Human_Rights_Watch-2005-147][^cite_note-149][^cite_note-Human_Rights_Watch-1998-150] while Kabul was heavily bombarded and partially destroyed by the fighting.[^cite_note-Human_Rights_Watch-1998-150] Several failed reconciliations and alliances occurred between different leaders.[^cite_note-151] The [Taliban](30635.md) emerged in September 1994 as a movement and militia of students (*talib*) from Islamic [madrassas (schools) in Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madrassas_in_Pakistan),[^cite_note-Human_Rights_Watch-1998-150][^cite_note-152] who soon had military support from Pakistan.[^cite_note-George_Washington_University-153]

Taking control of Kandahar city that year,[^cite_note-Human_Rights_Watch-1998-150] they conquered more territories until finally driving out the government of [Rabbani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burhanuddin_Rabbani) from Kabul in 1996,[^cite_note-154][^cite_note-155] where they established an [emirate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Emirate_of_Afghanistan_\(1996%E2%80%932001\)).[^cite_note-156] The Taliban were condemned internationally for the harsh enforcement of their interpretation of Islamic [sharia](28840.md) law, which resulted in the brutal treatment of many Afghans, especially [women](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taliban_treatment_of_women).[^cite_note-157][^cite_note-158] During their rule, the Taliban and their allies committed massacres against Afghan civilians, denied UN food supplies to starving civilians, and conducted a policy of [scorched earth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scorched_earth), burning vast areas of fertile land and destroying tens of thousands of homes.[^cite_note-165]

[After the fall of Kabul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Kabul_\(1992%E2%80%931996\)) to the Taliban, [Ahmad Shah Massoud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmad_Shah_Massoud) and [Abdul Rashid Dostum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdul_Rashid_Dostum) formed the [Northern Alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Alliance), later joined by others, to resist the Taliban. Dostum's forces were defeated by the Taliban during the [Battles of Mazar-i-Sharif](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battles_of_Mazar-i-Sharif_\(1997%E2%80%931998\)) in 1997 and 1998; Pakistan's Chief of Army Staff, [Pervez Musharraf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pervez_Musharraf), began sending thousands of Pakistani militants to help the Taliban defeat the Northern Alliance.[^cite_note-170] By 2000, the Northern Alliance only controlled 10% of the territory, cornered in the northeast. On 9 September 2001, Massoud was assassinated by two Arab [suicide attackers](18835454.md) in [Panjshir Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panjshir_Valley). Around 400,000 Afghans died in internal conflicts between 1990 and 2001.[^cite_note-171]

### US invasion and the Islamic Republic

Further information: [War in Afghanistan (2001–2021)](19666611.md), [Islamic Republic of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Republic_of_Afghanistan), [Taliban insurgency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taliban_insurgency), and [Fall of Kabul (2021)](68481047.md)

In October 2001, the [United States invaded Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_invasion_of_Afghanistan) to remove the Taliban from power after they refused to hand over [Osama bin Laden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Osama_bin_Laden), the prime suspect of the [September 11 attacks](5058690.md), who was a "guest" of the Taliban and was operating his [al-Qaeda](1921.md) network in Afghanistan.[^cite_note-172][^cite_note-173][^cite_note-174] The majority of Afghans supported the American invasion.[^cite_note-175][^cite_note-176] During the initial invasion, US and UK forces bombed al-Qaeda training camps, and later, in cooperation with the Northern Alliance, the Taliban regime came to an end.[^cite_note-177]



US troops and [Chinooks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boeing_CH-47_Chinook) in Afghanistan, 2008

In December 2001, after the Taliban government was overthrown, the [Afghan Interim Administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_Interim_Administration) under [Hamid Karzai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamid_Karzai) was formed. The [International Security Assistance Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Security_Assistance_Force) (ISAF) was established by the [United Nations Security Council](31956.md) to assist the [Karzai administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_Hamid_Karzai) and provide basic security.[^cite_note-178][^cite_note-179] By this time, after two decades of war as well as an acute [famine](51329.md) at the time, Afghanistan had one of the highest [infant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infant_mortality) and child mortality rates in the world, the lowest life expectancy, much of the population suffered from hunger and malnutrition,[^cite_note-180][^cite_note-181][^cite_note-182] and infrastructure was in ruins.[^cite_note-183] Many foreign donors started providing aid and assistance to rebuild the war-torn country.[^cite_note-184][^cite_note-185] As coalition troops entered Afghanistan to help the [rebuilding process](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reconstruction_in_Afghanistan),[^cite_note-186][^cite_note-187] the [Taliban began an insurgency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taliban_insurgency) to regain control. Afghanistan remained one of the poorest countries in the world because of a lack of foreign investment, [government corruption](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corruption_in_Afghanistan), and the Taliban insurgency.[^cite_note-188][^cite_note-189]

The Afghan government was able to establish some democratic structures, adopting a constitution in 2004 with the name [Islamic Republic of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Republic_of_Afghanistan). Attempts were made, often with the support of foreign donor countries, to improve the country's economy, healthcare, education, transport, and agriculture. ISAF forces also began to train the [Afghan National Security Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_National_Security_Forces). Following 2002, nearly five million [Afghans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_diaspora) were repatriated.[^cite_note-190] The number of NATO troops present in Afghanistan peaked at 140,000 in 2011,[^cite_note-191] dropping to approximately 16,000 in 2018.[^cite_note-192] In September 2014, [Ashraf Ghani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashraf_Ghani) became president after the [2014 Afghan presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Afghan_presidential_election), in which, for the first time in Afghanistan's history, power was democratically transferred.[^cite_note-193][^cite_note-194][^cite_note-195] On 28 December 2014, NATO formally ended ISAF combat operations and transferred full security responsibility to the Afghan government. The NATO-led [Operation Resolute Support](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resolute_Support_Mission) was formed the same day as a successor to ISAF.[^cite_note-196][^cite_note-197] Thousands of NATO troops remained in the country to train and advise Afghan government forces[^cite_note-198] and continue their fight against the Taliban.[^cite_note-199] A report titled *Body Count* concluded that 106,000–170,000 civilians had been killed as a result of the fighting in Afghanistan at the hands of all parties to the conflict.[^cite_note-200]



A map of Afghanistan showing the [2021 Taliban offensive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Taliban_offensive)&#x20;

On 19 February 2020, the [United States–Taliban deal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States%E2%80%93Taliban_deal) was made in Qatar. The deal was one of the critical events that caused the collapse of the Afghan National Security Forces (ANSF);[^cite_note-201] following the signing of the deal, the US dramatically reduced the number of air attacks and deprived the ANSF of a critical edge in fighting the [Taliban insurgency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taliban_insurgency), leading to the Taliban takeover of Kabul.[^cite_note-202]

### Second Taliban era (2021–present)

Further information: [Aftermath of the Afghanistan War (2001–2021)](70511823.md)

NATO Secretary General [Jens Stoltenberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jens_Stoltenberg) announced on 14 April 2021 that the alliance had agreed to start [withdrawing its troops from Afghanistan](66883576.md) by 1 May.[^cite_note-203] Soon after NATO troops began withdrawing, the Taliban launched an [offensive against the Afghan government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Taliban_offensive) and quickly advanced in front of collapsing Afghan government forces.[^cite_note-204][^cite_note-205] The Taliban [captured the capital city of Kabul](68481047.md) on 15 August 2021, after regaining control over a vast majority of Afghanistan.[^cite_note-206] Several foreign diplomats and Afghan government officials, including President Ashraf Ghani,[^cite_note-207] were evacuated from the country, with many Afghan civilians attempting to flee along with them.[^cite_note-208] On 17 August, first vice president [Amrullah Saleh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amrullah_Saleh) proclaimed himself caretaker president and announced the formation of an [anti-Taliban front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Resistance_Front_of_Afghanistan) with a reported 6,000+ troops[^cite_note-209][^cite_note-210] in the [Panjshir Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panjshir_Valley), along with [Ahmad Massoud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmad_Massoud).[^cite_note-211][^cite_note-212] However, by 6 September, the Taliban had taken control of most of [Panjshir Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panjshir_Province), with resistance fighters retreating to the mountains.[^cite_note-213] Clashes in the valley ceased in mid-September.[^cite_note-214]

According to the [Costs of War Project](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Costs_of_War_Project), 176,000 people were killed in the conflict, including 46,319 civilians, between 2001 and 2021.[^cite_note-215] According to the [Uppsala Conflict Data Program](18537936.md), at least 212,191 people were killed in the conflict.[^cite_note-216] Though the state of war in the country ended in 2021, armed conflict persists in some regions[^cite_note-217][^cite_note-218][^cite_note-219] amid [fighting between the Taliban and the local branch of the Islamic State](60786878.md), as well as an anti-Taliban [Republican insurgency](68498551.md).[^cite_note-220]



Taliban fighters in Kabul on a captured [Humvee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humvee) following the [2021 fall of Kabul](68481047.md)

The Taliban government is led by [supreme leader](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Head_of_the_Islamic_Emirate_of_Afghanistan) [Hibatullah Akhundzada](50633776.md)[^cite_note-221] and acting prime minister [Hasan Akhund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hasan_Akhund), who took office on 7 September 2021.[^cite_note-222][^cite_note-223] Akhund is one of the four founders of the Taliban[^cite_note-224] and was a deputy prime minister of the previous emirate; his appointment was seen as a compromise between moderates and hardliners.[^cite_note-225] A [new, all-male cabinet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cabinet_of_Afghanistan) was formed, which included [Abdul Hakim Haqqani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdul_Hakim_Haqqani) as minister of justice.[^cite_note-226][^cite_note-227] On 20 September 2021, [United Nations Secretary-General](162415.md) [António Guterres](471806.md) received a letter from acting [minister of foreign affairs](8980088.md) [Amir Khan Muttaqi](68480375.md) to formally claim Afghanistan's seat as a member state for their official spokesman in [Doha](26214389.md), [Suhail Shaheen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suhail_Shaheen). The United Nations did not recognize the previous Taliban government and chose to work with the then government-in-exile instead.[^cite_note-228]

Western nations suspended most of their humanitarian aid to Afghanistan following the Taliban's August 2021 takeover of the country; the [World Bank](36752.md) and [International Monetary Fund](15251.md) also halted their payments.[^cite_note-229][^cite_note-230] More than half of Afghanistan's 39 million people faced an acute [food shortage](216361.md) in October 2021.[^cite_note-231] Human Rights Watch reported on 11 November 2021 that Afghanistan was facing widespread [famine](51329.md) due to an economic and banking crisis.[^cite_note-232]

The Taliban initially significantly tackled corruption, improving on the [corruption perceptions index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corruption_Perceptions_Index) from 174th to 150th out of 180 countries from 2021 to 2022,[^cite_note-\:0-233] but dropping to 162nd in 2023.[^cite_note-234] The Taliban have also reportedly reduced bribery and extortion in public service areas.[^cite_note-\:0-233]



A Taliban official with Iranian Foreign Minister [Hossein Amir-Abdollahian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hossein_Amir-Abdollahian) in 2023

At the same time, the human rights situation in the country has deteriorated.[^cite_note-235] Following the 2001 invasion, more than 5.7 million refugees returned to Afghanistan;[^cite_note-236] however, in 2021, 2.6 million Afghans remained refugees, primarily in Iran and Pakistan, and another 4 million were internally displaced.[^cite_note-237]

In October 2023, the Pakistani government ordered the [expulsion of Afghans from Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Expulsion_of_Afghans_from_Pakistan).[^cite_note-238] Iran also decided to deport [Afghan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghans_in_Iran) nationals back to Afghanistan.[^cite_note-239] Taliban authorities condemned the deportations of Afghans as an "inhuman act".[^cite_note-240] Afghanistan faced a humanitarian crisis in late 2023.[^cite_note-241]

On 10 November 2024, Afghanistan's Foreign Ministry confirmed that Taliban representatives would attend the [2024 United Nations Climate Change Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_United_Nations_Climate_Change_Conference), marking the first time the country participated since the Taliban's return to power in 2021. Afghanistan had been barred from previous summits due to the lack of global recognition of the Taliban regime.[^cite_note-242] In May 2025, Iran ordered the [mass deportation of an estimated 4 million Afghan migrants and refugees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Afghan_deportation_from_Iran).[^cite_note-243]

The [2025–2026 hunger crisis in Afghanistan](81152082.md) is one of the world's most severe, with over 17 million people—one-third of the population—requiring urgent food assistance. Driven by persistent [drought](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drought_in_Afghanistan), economic collapse, and massive refugee returns, the crisis has left over 4.9 million children and women needing malnutrition treatment.[^cite_note-244]

The Taliban has been accused by Pakistan of officially supporting and providing safe haven to the [Pakistani Taliban](18953699.md) (TTP) and other militant groups that attack Pakistani soil, accusations which have been denied by the Taliban. Tensions between both countries led to a [direct war](82482902.md) in February 2026.[^cite_note-245][^cite_note-246]

## Geography

Main article: [Geography of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_Afghanistan)

Afghanistan is located in Southern-Central Asia.[^cite_note-247][^cite_note-248][^cite_note-249][^cite_note-250][^cite_note-251] The region centered in Afghanistan is considered the "crossroads of Asia",[^cite_note-252] and the country has had the nickname Heart of Asia.[^cite_note-253] The renowned [Urdu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urdu) poet [Allama Iqbal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allama_Iqbal) once wrote about the country:

> Asia is a body of water and earth, of which the Afghan nation is the heart. From its discord, the discord of Asia; and from its accord, the accord of Asia.

At over 652,864 km<sup>2</sup> (252,072 sq mi),[^cite_note-254] Afghanistan is the world's [41st largest country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_and_dependencies_by_area).[^cite_note-255] It is slightly bigger than France and smaller than Myanmar, and about the size of Texas in the United States. There is no coastline, as Afghanistan is [landlocked](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landlocked). Afghanistan shares its longest land border (the [Durand Line](203570.md)) with Pakistan to the east and south, followed by borders with Tajikistan to the northeast, Iran to the west, Turkmenistan to the north-west, Uzbekistan to the north, and China to the far northeast; India claims a border with Afghanistan through Pakistani-administered [Kashmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashmir).[^cite_note-256] Clockwise from south-west, Afghanistan shares borders with the [Sistan and Baluchestan Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sistan_and_Baluchestan_Province), South Khorasan Province, and [Razavi Khorasan Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Razavi_Khorasan_Province) of Iran; [Ahal Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahal_Region), [Mary Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mary_Region), and [Lebap Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lebap_Region) of Turkmenistan; [Surxondaryo Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surxondaryo_Region) of Uzbekistan; [Khatlon Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khatlon_Region) and [Gorno-Badakhshan Autonomous Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gorno-Badakhshan) of Tajikistan; [Xinjiang Uyghur Autonomous Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xinjiang) of China; and the [Gilgit-Baltistan territory](18807336.md), [Khyber Pakhtunkhwa province](21950.md), and [Balochistan province](4687.md) of Pakistan.[^cite_note-257]



Floodplain cultivation in the [Wakhan Corridor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wakhan_Corridor), [Pamir Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pamir_Mountains)

The geography in Afghanistan is varied, but is mostly mountainous and rugged, with some unusual mountain ridges accompanied by plateaus and river basins.[^cite_note-Fisher-2002-258] It is dominated by the [Hindu Kush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindu_Kush) range, the western extension of the [Himalayas](37966.md) that stretches to eastern [Tibet](31516.md) via the [Pamir Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pamir_Mountains) and [Karakoram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karakoram) Mountains in Afghanistan's far north-east. Most of the highest points are in the east, consisting of fertile mountain valleys, often considered part of the "[Roof of the World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roof_of_the_World)". The Hindu Kush ends at the west-central highlands, creating plains in the north and southwest, namely the [Turkestan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkestan) Plains and the [Sistan Basin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sistan_Basin); these two regions consist of rolling grasslands and semi-deserts, as well as hot, windy deserts, respectively.[^cite_note-259] Forests exist in the corridor between the provinces of [Nuristan](457267.md) and [Paktika](457294.md) (see [East Afghan montane conifer forests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Afghan_montane_conifer_forests)),[^cite_note-260] and [tundra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tundra) in the northeast. The country's highest point is [Noshaq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noshaq), at 7,492 m (24,580 ft) above sea level.[^cite_note-Factbook-261] The lowest point is located in [Jowzjan Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jowzjan_Province) along the Amu River bank, at 258 m (846 ft) above sea level.



The mountainous [topography](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Topography) of Afghanistan

Despite having numerous rivers and [reservoirs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_dams_and_reservoirs_in_Afghanistan), large parts of the country are dry. The [endorheic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Endorheic) Sistan Basin is one of the world's driest regions.[^cite_note-262] The [Amu Darya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amu_Darya) rises in the north of the Hindu Kush, while the nearby [Hari River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hari_\(Afghanistan\)) flows west towards [Herat](14128.md), and the [Arghandab River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arghandab_River) flows from the central region southwards. To the south and west of the Hindu Kush flow a number of streams that are tributaries of the [Indus River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indus_River),[^cite_note-Fisher-2002-258] such as the [Helmand River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helmand_River). The [Kabul River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kabul_River) flows in an easterly direction to the Indus, ending at the Indian Ocean.[^cite_note-afghanistans.com-1-263] Afghanistan receives heavy snow during the winter in the Hindu Kush and Pamir Mountains, and the melting snow in the spring season enters the [rivers, lakes, and streams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_rivers_of_Afghanistan).[^cite_note-264][^cite_note-265] However, two-thirds of the country's water flows into the neighboring countries of Iran, Pakistan, and [Turkmenistan](198149.md). As reported in 2010, the state needs more than US\$2 billion to rehabilitate its irrigation systems so that the water is properly managed.[^cite_note-266]

In Afghanistan, [forest cover](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forest_cover) accounts for approximately 2% of the total land area, equivalent to 1,208,440 hectares (ha) of forest in 2020, which remained unchanged since 1990. Of the naturally regenerating forest, 0% was reported to be [primary forest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Primary_forest) (consisting of native tree species with no clearly visible indications of human activity), and around 0% of the forest area was found within protected areas. For the year 2015, 100% of the forest area was reported to be under [public ownership](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_ownership).[^cite_note-267][^cite_note-268]

The northeastern Hindu Kush [mountain range](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mountain_range), located in and around the [Badakhshan Province](1282198.md) of Afghanistan, is situated in a [geologically active](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_environment#Geological_activity) area where earthquakes occur almost every year.[^cite_note-269] They can be deadly and destructive, causing [landslides](37208.md) in some parts or [avalanches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2009_Afghan_avalanches) during the winter.[^cite_note-270] In June 2022, a [destructive 5.9 earthquake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/June_2022_Afghanistan_earthquake) struck near the border with Pakistan, killing at least 1,150 people and sparking fears of a major humanitarian crisis.[^cite_note-271] On 7 October 2023, a [6.3 magnitude earthquake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/October_2023_Afghanistan_earthquake) struck northwest of Herat, killing over 1,400 people.[^cite_note-272]

### Climate



[Köppen climate map](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification) of Afghanistan

[^cite_note-273]

Afghanistan has a [continental climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continental_climate) with harsh winters in the [central highlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hazarajat), the glaciated northeast (around [Nuristan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuristan)), and the [Wakhan Corridor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wakhan_Corridor), where the average temperature in January is below −15 °C (5 °F) and can reach −26 °C (−15 °F),[^cite_note-Fisher-2002-258] and hot summers in the low-lying areas of the [Sistan Basin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sistan_Basin) of the southwest, the [Jalalabad](16274.md) basin in the east, and the [Turkestan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_Turkestan) plains along the [Amu River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amu_River) in the north, where temperatures average over 35 °C (95 °F) in July[^cite_note-Factbook-261][^cite_note-274] and can go over 43 °C (109 °F).[^cite_note-Fisher-2002-258] The country is generally [arid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arid) in the summers, with most rainfall falling between December and April. The lower areas of northern and western Afghanistan are the driest, with precipitation more common in the east. Although proximate to India, Afghanistan is mostly outside the [monsoon](57630.md) zone,[^cite_note-Fisher-2002-258] except the [Nuristan Province](457267.md), which occasionally receives summer monsoon rain.[^cite_note-275]

Although Afghanistan has contributed minimally to global [greenhouse gas emissions](9528025.md), it is one of the most vulnerable countries to [climate change](5042951.md) and least prepared to cope with its impacts.[^cite_note-276] [Climate change in Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_change_in_Afghanistan) is causing more frequent and severe droughts.[^cite_note-277][^cite_note-278] Severe drought conditions affect 25 of the country's 34 provinces, impacting over half the population.[^cite_note-\:43-279] These droughts cause [desertification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Desertification),[^cite_note-\:43-279][^cite_note-280] reduce [food](216361.md)[^cite_note-281] and [water security](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_security),[^cite_note-282] disrupt agriculture, and cause [internal displacement](427408.md).[^cite_note-actionaid.org-283] Extreme rainfall over short periods is also more likely, increasing the risk of [floods](50482.md) and [landslides](37208.md).[^cite_note-284] Due to rising temperatures, almost 14% of Afghanistan's glacier coverage was lost between 1990 and 2015,[^cite_note-285] increasing the risk of [glacial lake outburst floods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glacial_lake_outburst_flood).[^cite_note-286] By 2050, climate change could displace an additional 5 million people within Afghanistan.[^cite_note-actionaid.org-283][^cite_note-\:2-287]

### Biodiversity

Main article: [Wildlife of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wildlife_of_Afghanistan)



The [snow leopard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Snow_leopard) is the official national animal of Afghanistan.

Several types of mammals exist throughout Afghanistan. [Snow leopards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Snow_leopard) and [brown bears](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brown_bear) live in the high elevation [alpine tundra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alpine_tundra) regions. The [Marco Polo sheep](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marco_Polo_sheep) exclusively live in the Wakhan Corridor region of north-east Afghanistan. Foxes, [wolves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wolves), [otters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Otter), [deer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deer), [wild sheep](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wild_sheep), [lynx](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynx) and other big cats populate the mountain forest region of the east. In the semi-desert northern plains, wildlife include a variety of birds, [hedgehogs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hedgehog), [gophers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gopher), and large carnivores such as [jackals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_jackal) and [hyenas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Striped_hyena).[^cite_note-Gritzner-2009-288]

[Gazelles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gazelle), [wild pigs](52255.md) and jackals populate the [steppe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steppe) plains of the south and west, while [mongoose](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongoose) and cheetahs exist in the semi-desert south.[^cite_note-Gritzner-2009-288] [Marmots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marmot) and [ibex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibex) also live in the high mountains of Afghanistan, and [pheasants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pheasant) exist in some parts of the country.[^cite_note-289] The [Afghan hound](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_hound) is a native breed of dog known for its fast speed and its long hair; it is relatively known in the west.[^cite_note-Wahab-2007-290]

[Endemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Endemic) fauna of Afghanistan includes the [Afghan flying squirrel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_flying_squirrel), [Afghan snowfinch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_snowfinch), *[Paradactylodon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paradactylodon)* (or the "[Paghman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paghman) mountain salamander"), *[Stigmella kasyi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stigmella_kasyi)*, *[Vulcaniella kabulensis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vulcaniella_kabulensis)*, [Afghan leopard gecko](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_leopard_gecko), *[Wheeleria parviflorellus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wheeleria_parviflorellus)*, among others. Endemic flora include *[Iris afghanica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iris_afghanica)*. Afghanistan has a wide variety of birds despite its relatively arid climate – an estimated 460 species of which 235 breed within.[^cite_note-Wahab-2007-290]

The forest region of Afghanistan has vegetation such as [pine trees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pine_tree), [spruce trees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spruce_tree), [fir trees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fir_tree) and [larches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Larch), whereas the steppe grassland regions consist of [broadleaf trees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Broadleaf_tree), short grass, [perennial plants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perennial_plant) and [shrublands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shrubland). The colder high elevation regions are composed of hardy grasses and small flowering plants.[^cite_note-Gritzner-2009-288] Several regions are designated [protected areas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_protected_areas_of_Afghanistan); there are three [national parks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_parks): [Band-e Amir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Band-e_Amir), [Wakhan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wakhan_National_Park) and [Nuristan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuristan_National_Park). Afghanistan had a 2018 [Forest Landscape Integrity Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forest_Landscape_Integrity_Index) mean score of 8.85/10, ranking it 15th globally out of 172 countries.[^cite_note-291]

## Government and politics

Main articles: [Government of Afghanistan](68866725.md) and [Politics of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics_of_Afghanistan)



[The Arg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arg,_Kabul) (the Presidential palace) in Kabul, photographed in 2020

Following the effective collapse of the [Islamic Republic of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Republic_of_Afghanistan) during the [2021 Taliban offensive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Taliban_offensive), the Taliban declared the country an Islamic Emirate. A new caretaker government was announced on 7 September.[^cite_note-BBCHardliners-292] As of July 2025, only the [Russian Federation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Federation) has [recognized the Islamic Emirate of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recognition_of_the_Islamic_Emirate_of_Afghanistan) as the *[de jure](43594.md)* government of Afghanistan.[^cite_note-Trevelyan-293] In the [V-Dem Democracy Indices](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/V-Dem_Democracy_Indices), Afghanistan ranks amongst the lowest as a "closed autocracy," ranking at 176th and 177th out of 179 countries in electoral democracy and liberal democracy indices respectively.[^cite_note-j496-294] Afghanistan operates on a centralised [unitary form of government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unitary_state).[^cite_note-Unitary-295]

A traditional instrument of governance in Afghanistan is the *[loya jirga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loya_jirga)* (grand assembly), a Pashtun consultative meeting that was mainly organized for choosing a new [head of state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Head_of_state), adopting a new constitution, or to settle national or regional issue such as war.[^cite_note-296] Loya jirgas have been held since at least 1747,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarfield2012295-297] with [the most recent one](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghanistan_Ulema_gathering) occurring from June to July 2022.[^cite_note-298][^cite_note-299][^cite_note-300]

### Development of Taliban government

See also: [Akhund cabinet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akhund_cabinet)



[Hasan Akhund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hasan_Akhund)\
[Prime Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_Afghanistan)



[Sirajuddin Haqqani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sirajuddin_Haqqani)\
First [Deputy Leader](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deputy_Leader_of_the_Islamic_Emirate_of_Afghanistan) and [Interior Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interior_Minister_of_Afghanistan)



[Mullah Yaqoob](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mullah_Yaqoob)\
Second Deputy Leader and [Defense Minister](1289906.md)



[Abdul Ghani Baradar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdul_Ghani_Baradar)\
Third Deputy Leader and First [Deputy Prime Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deputy_Prime_Minister_of_Afghanistan)

On 17 August 2021, the leader of the Taliban-affiliated [Hezb-e-Islami Gulbuddin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hezb-e_Islami_Gulbuddin) party, [Gulbuddin Hekmatyar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulbuddin_Hekmatyar), met with both [Hamid Karzai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamid_Karzai), the former [President of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_Afghanistan), and [Abdullah Abdullah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdullah_Abdullah), the former chairman of the High Council for National Reconciliation and former [Chief Executive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chief_Executive_\(Afghanistan\)), in [Doha](26214389.md), Qatar, with the aim of forming a [national unity government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_unity_government).[^cite_note-301][^cite_note-302] President Ashraf Ghani, having fled the country during the Taliban advance to either Tajikistan or [Uzbekistan](31853.md), emerged in the United Arab Emirates and said that he supported such negotiations and was in talks to return to Afghanistan.[^cite_note-303][^cite_note-304] The [2004 constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2004_Constitution_of_Afghanistan) was essentially abolished on 15 August 2021, with the [overthrow and dissolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Taliban_offensive) of the Islamic Republic of Afghanistan by the [Taliban](30635.md). In August 2022, it was confirmed to the public that Supreme Leader [Hibatullah Akhundzada](50633776.md) had earlier formally abolished the 2004 Constitution on his authority.[^cite_note-305]

Hours after the final flight of American troops left Kabul on 30 August, a Taliban official interviewed said that a new government would likely be announced as early as Friday 3 September after [Jumu'ah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jumu'ah).
