It was added that Hibatullah Akhundzada would be officially named [Emir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Head_of_the_Islamic_Emirate_of_Afghanistan), with cabinet ministers being revealed at the [Arg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arg_\(Kabul\)) in an official ceremony. Abdul Ghani Baradar would be named [head of government](37570.md) as [Prime Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_Afghanistan), while other important positions would go to [Sirajuddin Haqqani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sirajuddin_Haqqani) and [Mullah Yaqoob](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mullah_Yaqoob). Beneath the supreme leader, day-to-day governance will be entrusted to the [cabinet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cabinet_of_Afghanistan#Islamic_Emirate_\(2021%E2%80%93present\)).[^cite_note-306]

In a report by CNN-News18, sources said the new government was going to be governed similarly to Iran with Hibatullah Akhundzada as supreme leader similar to the role of [Saayid Ali Khamenei](385653.md), and would be based out of [Kandahar](17260.md). Baradar or Yaqoob would be head of government as Prime minister. The government's ministries and agencies will be under a cabinet presided over by the Prime Minister. The Supreme Leader would preside over an [executive body](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Executive_\(government\)) known as the Supreme Council with anywhere from 11 to 72 members. [Abdul Hakim Haqqani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdul_Hakim_Haqqani) is likely to be promoted to [Chief justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chief_justice). According to the report, the new government will take place within the framework of an amended [1964 Constitution of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1964_Constitution_of_Afghanistan).[^cite_note-307] Government formation was delayed due to concerns about forming a broad-based government acceptable to the international community.[^cite_note-308] It was later added however that the Taliban's Rahbari Shura, the group's leadership council was divided between the hardline Haqqani Network and moderate Abdul Ghani Baradar over appointments needed to form an "inclusive" government. Reports claimed that this culminated in a skirmish which led to Baradar being injured and treated in Pakistan, however this was denied by Baradar himself.[^cite_note-309][^cite_note-310]

As of early September 2021 the Taliban were planning the cabinet to be men-only. Journalists and other human rights activists, mostly women, [protested](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Afghan_protests) in Herat and Kabul, calling for women to be included.[^cite_note-311] The [acting Cabinet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cabinet_of_Afghanistan#Islamic_Emirate_\(2021%E2%80%93present\)) announced on 7 September was men-only, and the [Ministry of Women's Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Women's_Affairs_\(Afghanistan\)) was abolished.[^cite_note-BBCHardliners-292] In a decree by Hibatullah Akhundzada on 15 August 2025, the description of the government as interim was dropped, with all ministers remaining in their posts, but on a permanent rather than acting basis.[^cite_note-312][^cite_note-313]

As of July 2025, only [Russia](25391.md) has recognized the Taliban government as the legitimate authorities of Afghanistan.[^cite_note-Trevelyan-293] The U.N has stated that recognition was impossible so long as restrictions on female education and employment remained.[^cite_note-\:1-314][^cite_note-315]

On 16 September 2024, the Taliban suspended [polio vaccination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polio_vaccination) campaigns in Afghanistan, as reported by the United Nations, posing a significant risk to global [polio eradication](9901652.md) efforts,[^cite_note-316] though multiple national and sub-national polio vaccination campaigns were subsequently carried out in 2025 and 2026.[^cite_note-317]

### Administrative divisions

Main articles: [Provinces of Afghanistan](412343.md) and [Districts of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Districts_of_Afghanistan)

Afghanistan is administratively divided into 34 provinces (*[wilayat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wilayat)*).[^cite_note-318] Each province has a governor and a capital. The country is further divided into nearly 400 provincial [districts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Districts_of_Afghanistan), each of which normally covers a city or several villages. Each district is represented by a district governor.

The [provincial governors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_current_governors_of_Afghanistan) are now appointed by the [Prime Minister of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_Afghanistan), and the district governors are selected by the provincial governors.[^cite_note-319] The provincial governors are representatives of the central government in Kabul and are responsible for all administrative and formal issues within their provinces. There are also provincial councils that are elected through direct and general elections for four years.[^cite_note-320] The functions of provincial councils are to take part in provincial development planning and to participate in the monitoring and appraisal of other provincial governance institutions.

According to article 140 of the constitution and the presidential decree on electoral law, mayors of cities should be elected through free and direct elections for a four-year term. In practice however, mayors are appointed by the government.[^cite_note-321]



Afghanistan is divided into [34 provinces](412343.md), which are further divided into a [number of districts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Districts_of_Afghanistan).

The 34 provinces in alphabetical order are:

### Foreign relations

Main article: [Foreign relations of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_relations_of_Afghanistan)

See also: [Ministry of Foreign Affairs (Afghanistan)](8980088.md)

Afghanistan became a member of the United Nations in 1946.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDupree1997642-322] Historically, Afghanistan had strong relations with Germany, one of the first countries to recognize Afghanistan's independence in 1919; the Soviet Union, which provided much aid and military training for Afghanistan's forces and includes the signing of a Treaty of Friendship in 1921 and 1978; and India, with which a friendship treaty was signed in 1950.[^cite_note-323] Relations with Pakistan have often been tense for various reasons such as the Durand Line border issue and alleged Pakistani involvement in Afghan insurgent groups.

The present Islamic Emirate of Afghanistan is currently [partially recognized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recognition_of_the_Islamic_Emirate_of_Afghanistan), but has had notable unofficial ties with China, Pakistan, and Qatar.[^cite_note-324][^cite_note-325] Under the previous Islamic Republic of Afghanistan, it enjoyed cordial relations with a number of [NATO](21133.md) and allied nations, particularly the [United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghanistan%E2%80%93United_States_relations), [Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghanistan%E2%80%93Canada_relations), [United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghanistan%E2%80%93United_Kingdom_relations), [Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghanistan%E2%80%93Germany_relations), Australia, and [Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghanistan%E2%80%93Turkey_relations). In 2012, the United States and the then-republic in Afghanistan signed their [Strategic Partnership Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US%E2%80%93Afghanistan_Strategic_Partnership_Agreement) in which Afghanistan became a [major non-NATO ally](3812445.md).[^cite_note-326] Such qualification was rescinded by US President [Joe Biden](145422.md) in July 2022.[^cite_note-327]

### Military

Main article: [Afghan Armed Forces](57836785.md)

The [Armed Forces of the Islamic Emirate of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armed_Forces_of_the_Islamic_Emirate_of_Afghanistan) captured a large amount of weapons, hardware, vehicles, aerocrafts, and equipment from the Afghan National Security Forces following the [2021 Taliban offensive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Taliban_offensive) and the [Fall of Kabul](68481047.md). The total value of the captured equipment has been estimated at US\$83 billion.[^cite_note-328][^cite_note-329]

### Human rights

Main article: [Human rights in Afghanistan](4317321.md)

Further information: [Women in Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women_in_Afghanistan), [LGBT rights in Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBT_rights_in_Afghanistan), [Treatment of women by the Taliban](31285.md), and [Afghan refugees](3657017.md)

Homosexuality is [taboo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taboo) in Afghan society;[^cite_note-330] according to the Penal Code, homosexual intimacy is punished by up to a year in prison.[^cite_note-331] Under [Sharia law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sharia_law) offenders can be [punished by death](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capital_punishment_for_homosexuality).[^cite_note-332][^cite_note-333] However, an ancient tradition involving male homosexual acts between children and older men (typically wealthy warlords or elite people) called *[bacha bazi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bacha_bazi)* persists albeit punishable by the [death penalty](5902.md) under Taliban law.[^cite_note-DS-334]

Religious minorities such as Sikhs,[^cite_note-335] Hindus,[^cite_note-336] and Christians have reportedly faced persecution.[^cite_note-337][^cite_note-338]

Since May 2022, all women in Afghanistan have been required by law to wear full-body coverings when in public (either a [burqa](165826.md) or an [abaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abaya) paired with a [niqāb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Niq%C4%81b), which leaves only the eyes uncovered).[^cite_note-339][^cite_note-340] First [Deputy Leader](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deputy_Leader_of_the_Islamic_Emirate_of_Afghanistan) [Sirajuddin Haqqani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sirajuddin_Haqqani) claimed the decree is only advisory and no form of [hijab](68301.md) is compulsory in Afghanistan,[^cite_note-341] though this contradicts the reality.[^cite_note-342] It has been speculated that there is a genuine internal policy division over women's rights between hardliners, including leader Hibatullah Akhundzada, and pragmatists, though they publicly present a united front.[^cite_note-343] Another decree was issued shortly after the first, requiring female TV presenters to cover their faces during broadcasts.[^cite_note-344] Since the Taliban takeover, suicides among women have become more common, and the country could now be one of the few where the rate of suicide among women surpasses that among men.[^cite_note-345][^cite_note-346]

In May 2022, the Taliban dissolved Afghanistan's Human Rights Commission along with four other government departments, citing the country's budget deficit.[^cite_note-347]

In January 2025, the [International Criminal Court](14880.md) issued two warrants against the [Taliban supreme leader](68497787.md) Haibatullah Akhundzada and the [Chief judge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chief_Justice_of_Afghanistan), Abdul Hakim Haqqani, for committing [crimes against humanity](61099.md) with the oppression and persecution of Afghan women and girls, and deprived of their freedom of movement, the rights to control their bodies, to education, and to a private and family life, while the alleged resistance and opposition were brutally suppressed with murder, imprisonment, torture, rape, and other forms of sexual violence, since 2021. ICC member states are obliged to arrest wanted persons if they are on their territory.[^cite_note-Taliban_warrent-348]

## Economy

Main article: [Economy of Afghanistan](9895.md)



Workers processing [pomegranates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pomegranate_production_in_Afghanistan) (*anaar*), for which Afghanistan is famous in Asia

Afghanistan's nominal GDP was \$20.1 billion in 2020, or \$81 billion by [purchasing power parity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Purchasing_power_parity) (PPP).[^cite_note-imf2-349] Its [GDP per capita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_\(PPP\)_per_capita) is \$2,459 (PPP) and \$611 by nominal.[^cite_note-imf2-349] Despite having \$1 trillion or more in mineral deposits,[^cite_note-350] it remains one of the world's [least developed countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Least_developed_countries). Its economic output per capita is among the [lowest of any country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_\(nominal\)_per_capita) as of 2026. Afghanistan's rough physical geography and its landlocked status has been cited as reasons why the country has always been among the least developed in the modern era – a factor where progress is also slowed by contemporary conflict and political instability.[^cite_note-Fisher-2002-258] The country imports over \$7 billion worth of goods but exports only \$784 million, mainly fruits and [nuts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nut_\(fruit\)). It has \$2.8 billion in [external debt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/External_debt).[^cite_note-Factbook-261] The service sector contributed the most to the GDP (55.9%) followed by agriculture (23%) and industry (21.1%).[^cite_note-351]

[Da Afghanistan Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Da_Afghanistan_Bank) serves as the central bank of the nation[^cite_note-352] and the [Afghani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_afghani) (AFN) is the national currency, with an exchange rate of about 75 Afghanis to 1 US dollar.[^cite_note-353] A number of local and foreign banks operate in the country, including the [Afghanistan International Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghanistan_International_Bank), [New Kabul Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Kabul_Bank), [Azizi Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azizi_Bank), [Pashtany Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pashtany_Bank), [Standard Chartered Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standard_Chartered_Bank), and the [First Micro Finance Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_MicroFinance_Bank-Afghanistan).



[Afghan rugs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_rugs) are one of Afghanistan's main exports.

In 2010, one of the main drivers for the economic recovery was the return of over 5 million [expatriates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_diaspora), who brought with them entrepreneurship and wealth-creating skills as well as much needed funds to start up businesses. Many Afghans got involved in construction, which was one of the largest industries in the country at the time.[^cite_note-354] Some of the major national construction projects include the \$35 billion New Kabul City next to the capital, the Aino Mena project in Kandahar, and the [Ghazi Amanullah Khan Town](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghazi_Amanullah_Khan_Town) near Jalalabad.[^cite_note-355][^cite_note-356][^cite_note-357] Similar development projects have also begun in [Herat](14128.md), [Mazar-e-Sharif](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mazar-e-Sharif), and other cities.[^cite_note-358] An estimated 400,000 people enter the labor market each year.[^cite_note-359]

Several small companies and factories began operating in different parts of the country, which not only provide revenues to the government but also create new jobs. Improvements to the business environment have resulted in more than \$1.5 billion in [telecom](33094374.md) investment and created more than 100,000 jobs since 2003.[^cite_note-360] [Afghan rugs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_rug) are becoming popular again, allowing many carpet dealers around the country to hire more workers; in 2016–17 it was the fourth most exported group of items.[^cite_note-361]

Afghanistan is a member of [WTO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WTO), [SAARC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SAARC), [ECO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_Cooperation_Organization), and [OIC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/OIC). It holds an observer status in [SCO](152422.md). In 2018, a majority of imports come from either Iran, China, Pakistan and Kazakhstan, while 84% of exports are to Pakistan and India.[^cite_note-362]

Since the Taliban's takeover of the country in August 2021, the United States has [frozen about \$9 billion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_frozen_assets) in assets belonging to the [Afghan central bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Da_Afghanistan_Bank),[^cite_note-363] blocking the Taliban from accessing billions of dollars held in US bank accounts.[^cite_note-364][^cite_note-365]

The GDP of Afghanistan is estimated to have dropped by 20% following the Taliban return to power. Following this, after months of free-fall, the Afghan economy began stabilizing, as a result of the Taliban's restrictions on smuggled imports, limits on banking transactions, and [UN aid](78868098.md). In 2023, the Afghan economy began seeing signs of revival. This has also been followed by stable exchange rates, low inflation, stable revenue collection, and the rise of trade in exports.[^cite_note-366] In the third quarter of 2023, the [Afghani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_afghani) rose to be the best performing currency in the world, climbing over 9% against the [US dollar](18717338.md).[^cite_note-367]

### Agriculture



Afghan [saffron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saffron)

Agricultural production is the backbone of Afghanistan's economy[^cite_note-368] and has traditionally dominated the economy, employing about 40% of the workforce as of 2018.[^cite_note-369] The country is known for producing [pomegranates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pomegranate_production_in_Afghanistan), grapes, apricots, melons, and several other fresh and dry fruits. Afghanistan also became the world's top producer of [cannabis](1481886.md) in 2010.[^cite_note-370] In March 2023, however, cannabis production was banned by a decree from Hibatullah Akhundzada.[^cite_note-371]

[Saffron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saffron), the most expensive spice, grows in Afghanistan, particularly [Herat Province](312559.md). In recent years, there has been an uptick in saffron production, which authorities and farmers are using to try to replace poppy cultivation. Between 2012 and 2019, the saffron cultivated and produced in Afghanistan was consecutively ranked the world's best by the International Taste and Quality Institute.[^cite_note-372][^cite_note-373] Production hit record high in 2019 (19,469 kg of saffron), and one kilogram is sold domestically between \$634 and \$1147.[^cite_note-374]

The availability of cheap diesel-powered water pumps imported from China and Pakistan, and in the 2010s, of cheap solar power to pump water, resulted in expansion of agriculture and population in the southwestern deserts of Afghanistan in [Kandahar](457241.md), [Helmand](457235.md) and [Nimruz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nimruz_Province) provinces in the 2010s. Wells have gradually been deepened, but water resources are limited. Opium is the major crop, but as of 2022, was under attack by the new Taliban government which, to suppress opium production, was systematically suppressing water pumping.[^cite_note-375][^cite_note-376] In a 2023 report, poppy cultivation in southern Afghanistan was reduced by over 80% as a result of Taliban campaigns to stop its use toward opium. This included a 99% reduction of opium growth in the Helmand Province.[^cite_note-377] In November 2023, a U.N report showed that in the entirety of Afghanistan, poppy cultivation dropped by over 95%, removing it from its place as being the world's largest opium producer.[^cite_note-378][^cite_note-379]

### Mining

Main article: [Mining in Afghanistan](17967521.md)



[Lapis lazuli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lapis_lazuli) stones

The country's natural resources include: coal, copper, iron ore, [lithium](17561.md), [uranium](31743.md), [rare earth elements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rare_earth_element), [chromite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chromite), gold, [zinc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zinc), [talc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talc), [barite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barite), [sulfur](27127.md), lead, [marble](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marble), precious and [semi-precious stones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semi-precious_stones), natural gas, and petroleum.[^cite_note-peters2007-380][^cite_note-bgs-381] In 2010, US and Afghan government officials estimated that untapped mineral deposits located in 2007 by the [US Geological Survey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US_Geological_Survey) are worth at least \$1 trillion.[^cite_note-bbcminerals-382]

[Michael E. O'Hanlon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_E._O'Hanlon) of the [Brookings Institution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brookings_Institution) estimated that if Afghanistan generates about \$10 billion per year from its [mineral deposits](17967521.md), its [gross national product](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gross_national_product) would double and provide long-term funding for critical needs.[^cite_note-383] The [United States Geological Survey](23814944.md) (USGS) estimated in 2006 that northern Afghanistan has an average 460 million m<sup>3</sup> (2.9 billion bbl) of [crude oil](23195.md), 440 billion m<sup>3</sup> (15.7 trillion cu ft) of natural gas, and 67 billion L (562 million US bbl) of [natural gas liquids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_gas_liquids).[^cite_note-384] In 2011, Afghanistan signed an oil exploration contract with [China National Petroleum Corporation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China_National_Petroleum_Corporation) (CNPC) for the development of three oil fields along the Amu Darya river in the north.[^cite_note-385]

The country has significant amounts of [lithium](17561.md), copper, gold, coal, iron ore, and other [minerals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minerals).[^cite_note-peters2007-380][^cite_note-bgs-381][^cite_note-386] The [Khanashin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khanashin) [carbonatite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbonatite) in Helmand Province contains 1,000,000 tonnes (980,000 [long tons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Long_ton); 1,100,000 [short tons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Short_ton)) of [rare earth elements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rare_earth_element).[^cite_note-387] In 2007, a 30-year lease was granted for the [Aynak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mes_Aynak#Copper_Mine) copper mine to the [China Metallurgical Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China_Metallurgical_Group) for \$3 billion,[^cite_note-388] making it the biggest foreign investment and private business venture in Afghanistan's history.[^cite_note-389] The state-run [Steel Authority of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steel_Authority_of_India) won the mining rights to develop the huge [Hajigak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hajigak_Pass) iron ore deposit in central Afghanistan.[^cite_note-390] Government officials estimate that 30% of the country's untapped mineral deposits are worth at least \$1 trillion.[^cite_note-bbcminerals-382] One official asserted that "this will become the backbone of the Afghan economy" and a Pentagon memo stated that Afghanistan could become the "Saudi Arabia of lithium".[^cite_note-391] The lithium reserves of 21 Mio. tons could amount to the ones of [Bolivia](3462.md), which is currently viewed as the country with the largest lithium reserves.[^cite_note-Hosp-2021-392] Other larger deposits are the ones of [bauxite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bauxite) and [cobalt](24580536.md).[^cite_note-Hosp-2021-392]

Access to [biocapacity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biocapacity) in Afghanistan is lower than world average. In 2016, Afghanistan had 0.43 global hectares[^cite_note-footprintdata-393] of biocapacity per person within its territory, much less than the world average of 1.6 global hectares per person.[^cite_note-394] In 2016 Afghanistan used 0.73 global hectares of biocapacity per person—their [ecological footprint](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecological_footprint) of consumption. This means they use just under double as much biocapacity as Afghanistan contains. As a result, Afghanistan is running a biocapacity deficit.[^cite_note-footprintdata-393]

In September 2023, the [Taliban](30635.md) signed mining contracts worth \$6.5 billion, with extractions based on gold, iron, lead, and zinc in the provinces of Herat, Ghor, Logar, and Takhar.[^cite_note-395]

### Energy

Main article: [Energy in Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_in_Afghanistan)

See also: [Renewable energy in Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renewable_energy_in_Afghanistan)



Afghanistan electricity supply (1980–2019)

According to the [World Bank](36752.md), 98% of the rural population have access to electricity in 2018, up from 28% in 2008.[^cite_note-396] Overall the figure stands at 98.7%.[^cite_note-397] As of 2016, Afghanistan produces 1,400 [megawatts](21347693.md) of power, but still imports the majority of electricity via transmission lines from Iran and the Central Asian states.[^cite_note-398] The majority of electricity production is via [hydropower](14073.md), helped by the amount of rivers and streams that flow from the mountains.[^cite_note-399] However electricity is not always reliable and blackouts happen, including in Kabul.[^cite_note-Afghanistan_Analysts_Network_–_English-2015-400] In recent years an increasing number of [solar](13690575.md), [biomass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biomass) and wind power plants have been constructed.[^cite_note-401] Currently under development are the [CASA-1000](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CASA-1000) project which will transmit electricity from Kyrgyzstan and Tajikistan, and the [Turkmenistan-Afghanistan-Pakistan-India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkmenistan-Afghanistan-Pakistan-India_pipeline) (TAPI) gas pipeline.[^cite_note-Afghanistan_Analysts_Network_–_English-2015-400] Power is managed by the [Da Afghanistan Breshna Sherkat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Da_Afghanistan_Breshna_Sherkat?action=edit\&redlink=1) (DABS, Afghanistan Electricity Company).

Important dams include the [Kajaki Dam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kajaki_Dam), [Dahla Dam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dahla_Dam), and the [Sardeh Band Dam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sardeh_Band_Dam).[^cite_note-afghanistans.com-1-263]

### Tourism

Main article: [Tourism in Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tourism_in_Afghanistan)



[Band-e Amir National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Band-e_Amir_National_Park)

Tourism is a small industry in Afghanistan due to security issues. Nevertheless, some 20,000 foreign tourists visit the country annually as of 2016.[^cite_note-402] In particular an important region for domestic and international tourism is the picturesque [Bamyan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bamyan) Valley, which includes lakes, canyons and historical sites, helped by the fact it is in a safe area away from insurgent activity.[^cite_note-403][^cite_note-404] Smaller numbers visit and trek in regions such as the [Wakhan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wakhan) Valley, which is also one of the world's most remote communities.[^cite_note-405] From the late 1960s onwards, Afghanistan was a popular stop on the famous [hippie trail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hippie_trail), attracting many Europeans and Americans. Coming from Iran, the trail traveled through various Afghan provinces and cities including [Herat](14128.md), [Kandahar](17260.md) and [Kabul](16826.md) before crossing to northern Pakistan, northern India, and [Nepal](171166.md).[^cite_note-406][^cite_note-407] Tourism peaked in 1977, the year before the start of political instability and armed conflict.[^cite_note-408]



The [Minaret of Jam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minaret_of_Jam), a UNESCO [World Heritage Site](44940.md)

The city of [Ghazni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghazni) has significant history and historical sites, and together with [Bamyan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bamyan) city have in recent years been voted Islamic Cultural Capital and South Asia Cultural Capital respectively.[^cite_note-409] The cities of [Herat](14128.md), [Kandahar](17260.md), [Balkh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balkh), and [Zaranj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zaranj) are also very historic. The [Minaret of Jam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minaret_of_Jam) in the [Hari River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hari_River,_Afghanistan) valley is a [UNESCO World Heritage Site](44940.md). A cloak reputedly worn by Islam's prophet [Muhammad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad) is kept in the [Shrine of the Cloak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shrine_of_the_Cloak) in Kandahar, a city founded by [Alexander the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_the_Great) and the first capital of Afghanistan. The [citadel of Alexander](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Citadel_of_Alexander) in the western city of Herat has been renovated in recent years and is a popular attraction. In the north of the country is the [Shrine of Ali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shrine_of_Ali), believed by many to be the location where [Ali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ali) was buried.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDupree1997115-410] The [National Museum of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Museum_of_Afghanistan) in Kabul hosts a large number of Buddhist, [Bactrian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bactria) Greek and early Islamic antiquities; the museum suffered greatly by civil war but has been slowly restoring since the early 2000s.[^cite_note-411]

Unexpectedly, tourism has seen improvement in Afghanistan following the Taliban takeover. Active efforts by the Taliban encouraged tourism to increase from 691 tourists in 2021, to 2,300 in 2022, to 5,200 in 2023, with some estimates of between 7,000 and 10,000.[^cite_note-412][^cite_note-413][^cite_note-414] This is, however, threatened by the [Islamic State – Khorasan Province](48029263.md), who took responsibility for attacks on tourists, such as the [2024 Bamyan shooting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Bamyan_shooting).[^cite_note-415]

### Communication

Main article: [Communications in Afghanistan](6684.md)

Telecommunication services in Afghanistan are provided by [Afghan Telecom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_Telecom), [Afghan Wireless](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_Wireless), [Etisalat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Etisalat), [MTN Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MTN_Group), and [Roshan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roshan_\(telco\)). The country uses its own space [satellite](27683.md) called [Afghansat 1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghansat_1), which provides services to millions of phone, internet, and television subscribers. By 2001 following years of civil war, telecommunications was virtually a non-existent sector, but by 2016 it had grown to a \$2 billion industry, with 22 million mobile phone subscribers and 5 million internet users. The sector employs at least 120,000 people nationwide.[^cite_note-416]

### Transportation

Main article: [Transport in Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transport_in_Afghanistan)

See also: [List of airports in Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_airports_in_Afghanistan) and [Rail transport in Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rail_transport_in_Afghanistan)



The [Salang Tunnel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salang_Tunnel)

Due to Afghanistan's geography, transport between various parts of the country has historically been difficult. The backbone of Afghanistan's road network is [Highway 1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Highway_1_\(Afghanistan\)), often called the "Ring Road", which extends for 2,210 kilometres (1,370 mi) and connects five major cities: Kabul, Ghazni, Kandahar, Herat and Mazar-i-Sharif,[^cite_note-417] with spurs to Kunduz and Jalalabad and various border crossings, while skirting around the mountains of the Hindu Kush.[^cite_note-418]

The Ring Road is crucially important for domestic and international trade and the economy.[^cite_note-419] A key portion of the Ring Road is the [Salang Tunnel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salang_Tunnel), completed in 1964, which facilitates travel through the Hindu Kush mountain range and connects northern and southern Afghanistan.[^cite_note-420] It is the only land route that connects Central Asia to the [Indian subcontinent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_subcontinent).[^cite_note-421] Several mountain passes allow travel between the Hindu Kush in other areas. Serious traffic accidents are common on Afghan roads and highways, particularly on the [Kabul–Kandahar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kabul%E2%80%93Kandahar_Highway) and the [Kabul–Jalalabad Road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kabul%E2%80%93Jalalabad_Road).[^cite_note-422] Traveling by bus in Afghanistan remains dangerous due to militant activities.[^cite_note-423]



An [Ariana Afghan Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ariana_Afghan_Airlines) [Airbus A310](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Airbus_A310) in 2006

Air transport in Afghanistan is provided by the national carrier, [Ariana Afghan Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ariana_Afghan_Airlines),[^cite_note-424] and by the private company [Kam Air](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kam_Air). Airlines from a number of countries also provide flights in and out of the country. These include [Air India](26457880.md), [Emirates](142715.md), [Gulf Air](377403.md), [Iran Aseman Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran_Aseman_Airlines), [Pakistan International Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan_International_Airlines), and [Turkish Airlines](167925.md). The country has four international airports: [Kabul International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kabul_International_Airport) (formerly Hamid Karzai International Airport), [Kandahar International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kandahar_International_Airport), [Herat International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herat_International_Airport), and [Mazar-e Sharif International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mazar-e_Sharif_International_Airport). Including domestic airports, there are 43.[^cite_note-Factbook-261] [Bagram Air Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bagram_Air_Base) is a major military airfield.

The country has three rail links: one, a 75-kilometre (47 mi) line from [Mazar-i-Sharif](20517.md) to the [Uzbekistan border](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghanistan%E2%80%93Uzbekistan_Friendship_Bridge);[^cite_note-425] a 10-kilometre (6.2 mi) long line from [Toraghundi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toraghundi) to the [Turkmenistan](198149.md) border (where it continues as part of [Turkmen Railways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkmen_Railways)); and a short link from [Aqina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aqina) across the Turkmen border to [Kerki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kerki), which is planned to be extended further across Afghanistan.[^cite_note-426] These lines are used for freight only and there is no passenger service. A rail line between [Khaf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khaf,_Iran), Iran and [Herat](14128.md), western Afghanistan, intended for both freight and passengers, was under construction as of 2019.[^cite_note-427][^cite_note-428] About 125 kilometres (78 mi) of the line will lie on the Afghan side.[^cite_note-429][^cite_note-430]

Private vehicle ownership has increased substantially since the early 2000s. Taxis are yellow and consist of both cars and [auto rickshaws](593765.md).[^cite_note-431] In rural Afghanistan, villagers often use donkeys, [mules](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mule) or horses to transport or carry goods. Camels are primarily used by the Kochi nomads.[^cite_note-Wahab-2007-290] Bicycles are popular throughout Afghanistan.[^cite_note-432]

## Demographics

Main article: [Demographics of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Afghanistan)

Further information: [Afghans](2235.md) and [Afghan diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_diaspora)



A Cold War-era CIA map showing traditional Afghan tribal territories. [Pashtun tribes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pashtun_tribes) form the world's largest tribal society.

[^cite_note-433]

### Population

The population of Afghanistan was estimated at 35.7 million as of 2024 by the Afghanistan [National Statistics and Information Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Statistics_and_Information_Authority),[^cite_note-NSIA_pop-29] whereas the UN estimates over 42.0 million.[^cite_note-UN_pop-34] In 1979 the total population was reported to be about 15.5 million.[^cite_note-434] About 25.3% are [urbanite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_area), 70.4% live in rural areas, and the remaining 4.3% are nomadic.[^cite_note-NSIA_pop-29] An additional 3 million or so Afghans are temporarily housed in neighboring [Pakistan](22663356.md) and [Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghans_in_Iran), most of whom were born and raised in those two countries. As of 2013, Afghanistan was the largest refugee-producing country in the world, a title held for 32 years.

The current population growth rate is 2.37%,[^cite_note-Factbook-261] one of the highest in the world outside of Africa. This population is expected to reach 82 million by 2050 if current population trends continue.[^cite_note-435] The population of Afghanistan increased steadily until the 1980s, when civil war caused millions to flee to other countries such as Pakistan.[^cite_note-436] Millions have since returned and the war conditions contribute to the country having the highest fertility rate outside Africa.[^cite_note-437] Afghanistan's healthcare has recovered since the turn of the century, causing falls in infant mortality and increases in life expectancy, although it has the lowest life expectance of any country outside Africa. This (along with other factors such as returning refugees) caused rapid population growth in the 2000s that has only recently started to slow down. The [Gini coefficient](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gini_coefficient) in 2008 was 27.8.[^cite_note-438]

#### Fertility rate

Afghanistan 2024 [total fertility rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Total_fertility_rate) has been estimated at 4.4.[^cite_note-439] In 2022 it was 4.5, about twice the world average rate.[^cite_note-440] The rate has fallen since the early 1980s.[^cite_note-441]

### Urbanization

For a more comprehensive list, see [List of cities in Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_cities_in_Afghanistan).

As estimated by the CIA World Factbook, 26% of the population was urbanized as of 2020. This is one of the lowest figures in the world; in Asia it is only higher than [Cambodia](334751.md), [Nepal](171166.md) and [Sri Lanka](26750.md). Urbanization has increased rapidly, particularly in the capital [Kabul](16826.md), due to returning refugees from Pakistan and Iran after 2001, internally displaced people, and rural migrants.[^cite_note-442] Urbanization in Afghanistan is different from typical urbanization in that it is centered on just a few cities.[^cite_note-aljazeera.com-443]

Kabul is the largest city, with a population of 5 million.[^cite_note-NSIA_pop-29] The other large cities are located generally in the "ring" around the Central Highlands, namely [Kandahar](17260.md) in the south, [Herat](14128.md) in the west, [Mazar-i-Sharif](20517.md), [Kunduz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kunduz) in the north, and [Jalalabad](16274.md) in the east.

### Ethnicity and languages

Main articles: [Ethnic groups in Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_groups_in_Afghanistan) and [Languages of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_Afghanistan)

See also: [Afghans](2235.md)



Ethnolinguistic map of Afghanistan (2001)

[Afghans](2235.md) are divided into several [ethnolinguistic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnolinguistic_group) groups. The last ethnic census in Afghanistan was conducted in 1979, and was itself incomplete. Due to the [ongoing conflict](27576770.md) in the country, no official census has been conducted since.[^cite_note-Population_Matters2-445][^cite_note-reliefweb.int2-446] Afghanistan is made of minority ethnic groups, and no ethnic group make-up 50% or more to be called the majority. The [Pashtuns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pashtuns) forming between 37–42% of the total population,[^cite_note-mrg-447][^cite_note-Asia_Foundation_2021-448][^cite_note-Asia_Foundation_2017-449][^cite_note-ABCBBCARD2-450][^cite_note-Asia_Foundation_2018-451] followed by [Tajiks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tajiks) who comprise about 27%.[^cite_note-Population_Matters-452][^cite_note-timesofindia-453][^cite_note-454][^cite_note-reliefweb.int-455] The other two major ethnic groups are the [Hazaras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hazaras) and [Uzbeks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uzbeks), each at 9%. A further 10 other ethnic groups are recognized and each are represented in the [Afghan National Anthem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_National_Anthem).[^cite_note-456]

[Dari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dari) and [Pashto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pashto) are the [official languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Official_language) of Afghanistan;[^cite_note-457] [bilingualism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bilingualism) is very common.[^cite_note-458] Dari, which is also referred to as Eastern Persian as it is a variety of and mutually intelligible with [Persian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_language) (and very often called '[Farsi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Farsi)' by some Afghans like in [Iran](14653.md)), functions as the [*lingua franca*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lingua_franca) throughout the country.[^cite_note-459] Native speakers of Dari, of any ethnicity, are sometimes called [Farsiwans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Farsiwan).[^cite_note-460] Pashto is the native tongue of the [Pashtuns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pashtuns), although many of them are also fluent in Dari. Despite the Pashtuns having been dominant in Afghan politics for centuries, Dari remained the preferred language for government and bureaucracy.[^cite_note-461] According to [CIA World Factbook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_World_Factbook), Dari Persian is spoken by 78% ([L1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_language) + [L2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_language)) and functions as the country's *lingua franca*, while Pashto is spoken by 50%, [Uzbek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uzbek_language) 10%, English 5%, [Turkmen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkmen_language) 2%, [Urdu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urdu) 2%, [Pashayi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pashayi_languages) 1%, [Nuristani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuristani_languages) 1%, [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic) 1%, and [Balochi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balochi_language) 1% (2021 est). Data represent the most widely spoken languages; shares sum to more than 100% because there is much bilingualism in the country and because respondents were allowed to select more than one language. There are a number of smaller regional languages, including Uzbek, Turkmen, Balochi, Pashayi, and Nuristani.[^cite_note-Factbook-261]

When it comes to foreign languages among the populace, many are able to speak or understand [Hindustani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindustani_language) ([Urdu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urdu)-[Hindi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindi)), partly due to returning [Afghan refugees](22663356.md) from [Pakistan](23235.md) and the popularity of [Bollywood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bollywood) films respectively.[^cite_note-Afroz-2013-462] English is also understood by some of the population,[^cite_note-463] and has been gaining popularity as of the 2000s.[^cite_note-464] Some Afghans retain some ability in Russian, which was [taught in public schools during the 1980s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_occupation_of_Afghanistan).[^cite_note-Afroz-2013-462]

### Religion

Main article: [Religion in Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_Afghanistan)



[Blue Mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_Mosque_\(Mazar-i-Sharif\)) in [Mazar-i-Sharif](20517.md) is the largest mosque in Afghanistan.

The CIA estimated in 2009 that 99.7% of the Afghan population was Muslim[^cite_note-Factbook-261] and most are thought to adhere to the [Sunni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni) [Hanafi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hanafi) school.[^cite_note-The_Swedish_Committee_for_Afghanistan_(SCA)-465] According to [Pew Research Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Research_Center), as much as 90% are of the Sunni denomination, 7% [Shia](26961.md) and 3% [non-denominational](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-denominational_Muslim).[^cite_note-466] Conversion away from Islam is persecuted, limiting religious freedom.[^cite_note-m210-467]

Afghan [Sikhs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikhism_in_Afghanistan) and [Hindus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism_in_Afghanistan) are also found in certain major cities (namely Kabul, Jalalabad, Ghazni, Kandahar)[^cite_note-468][^cite_note-469] accompanied by gurdwaras and mandirs.[^cite_note-470] According to [Deutsche Welle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deutsche_Welle) in September 2021, 250 remain in the country after 67 were evacuated to India.[^cite_note-471]

There was a small [Jewish community in Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_Afghanistan), living mainly in Herat and Kabul. Over the years, this small community was forced to leave due to decades of warfare and religious persecution. By the end of the twentieth century, nearly the entire community had emigrated to Israel and the United States, with one known exception, Herat-born [Zablon Simintov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zablon_Simintov). He remained for years, being the caretaker of the only remaining Afghan synagogue. He left the country for the US after the second Taliban takeover. A woman who left shortly after him has since been identified as the likely last Jew in Afghanistan.[^cite_note-472][^cite_note-473][^cite_note-474]

[Afghan Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_Christians), who number 500–8,000, practice their faith secretly due to intense societal opposition, and there are no public churches.[^cite_note-475][^cite_note-476]

### Education

Main article: [Education in Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_Afghanistan)



UNESCO Institute of Statistics Afghanistan Literacy Rate among population aged 15+ (1980–2018)

[Education in Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_Afghanistan) is overseen by the [Ministry of Education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Education_\(Afghanistan\)) and the [Ministry of Higher Education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Higher_Education_\(Afghanistan\)). There are over 16,000 schools in the country and roughly 9 million students. Of this, about 60% are males and 40% females. However, the new regime has thus far forbidden female teachers and female students from returning to secondary schools.[^cite_note-477][^cite_note-478] Over 174,000 students are enrolled in different [universities around the country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_universities_in_Afghanistan). About 21% of these are females.[^cite_note-479] Former Education Minister [Ghulam Farooq Wardak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghulam_Farooq_Wardak) had stated that construction of 8,000 schools is required for the remaining children who are deprived of [formal learning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Formal_learning).[^cite_note-480] As of 2018 the literacy rate of the population age 15 and older is 43.02% (males 55.48% and females 29.81%).[^cite_note-481]

The top universities in Afghanistan are the [American University of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_University_of_Afghanistan) (AUAF) followed by [Kabul University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kabul_University) (KU), both of which are located in Kabul. The [National Military Academy of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Military_Academy_of_Afghanistan), modeled after the [United States Military Academy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Military_Academy) at West Point, was a four-year military development institution dedicated to graduating officers for the [Afghan Armed Forces](57836785.md). The [Afghan Defense University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_Defense_University) was constructed near [Qargha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qargha) in Kabul. Major universities outside of Kabul include [Kandahar University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kandahar_University) in the south, [Herat University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herat_University) in the northwest, [Balkh University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balkh_University) and [Kunduz University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kunduz_University) in the north, [Nangarhar University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nangarhar_University) and [Khost University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khost_University) in the east.

After the Taliban regained power in 2021, it became unclear to what extent female education would continue in the country. In March 2022, after they had been closed for some time, it was announced that secondary education would be reopened shortly. However, shortly before reopening, the order was rescinded and schools for older girls remained closed.[^cite_note-482] Despite the ban, six provinces, [Balkh](457218.md), [Kunduz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kunduz_Province), [Jowzjan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jowzjan_Province), [Sar-I-Pul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sar-e_Pol_Province), [Faryab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Faryab_Province), and the [Day Kundi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daykundi_Province), still allow girls' schools from grade 6 and up.[^cite_note-483][^cite_note-484] In December 2023, investigations were being held by the United Nations on the claim that Afghan girls of all ages were allowed to study at religious schools.[^cite_note-485] As of November 2024, some parts of the country allow women to attend religious schools to pursue dentistry, nursing, and other subjects.[^cite_note-486]

### Health

Main article: [Health in Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_in_Afghanistan)



The [Daoud Khan Military Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daoud_Khan_Military_Hospital) in Kabul is one of the largest [hospitals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_hospitals_in_Afghanistan) in Afghanistan.

According to the [Human Development Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_Development_Index), Afghanistan is the [15th least developed country in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_Human_Development_Index). The average [life expectancy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_life_expectancy) is estimated to be around 60 years.[^cite_note-487][^cite_note-UNESCO-2016-488] The country's [maternal mortality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maternal_mortality) rate is 396 deaths/100,000 live births and its [infant mortality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infant_mortality) rate is 66[^cite_note-UNESCO-2016-488] to 112.8 deaths in every 1,000 live births.[^cite_note-Factbook-261] The [Ministry of Public Health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Public_Health_\(Afghanistan\)) plans to cut the infant mortality rate to 400 for every 100,000 live births before 2020. The country has more than 3,000 [midwives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midwifery), with an additional 300 to 400 being trained each year.[^cite_note-489]

There are over 100 [hospitals in Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hospitals_in_Afghanistan),[^cite_note-490] with the most advanced treatments being available in Kabul. The [French Medical Institute for Children](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Medical_Institute_for_Children) and [Indira Gandhi Children's Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indira_Gandhi_Children's_Hospital) in Kabul are the leading [children's hospitals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Children's_hospital) in the country. Some of the other leading hospitals in Kabul include the [Jamhuriat Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jamhuriat_Hospital) and [Jinnah Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jinnah_Hospital_\(Kabul\)).[^cite_note-491] In spite of all this, many Afghans travel to Pakistan and India for advanced treatment.

It was reported in 2006 that nearly 60% of the Afghan population lives within a two-hour walk of the nearest health facility.[^cite_note-492] The [disability](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disability) rate is also high in Afghanistan due to the decades of war.[^cite_note-493] It was reported recently that about 80,000 people are missing limbs.[^cite_note-494][^cite_note-495] Non-governmental charities such as [Save the Children](436885.md) and [Mahboba's Promise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahboba's_Promise) assist orphans in association with governmental structures.[^cite_note-496]

## Culture

Main article: [Culture of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Afghanistan)



An Afghan family near [Kholm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kholm,_Afghanistan), 1939 – most Afghans are tribal.

[Afghans](2235.md) have both common cultural features and those that differ between the regions of Afghanistan, each with distinctive cultures partly as a result of geographic obstacles that divide the country.[^cite_note-Fisher-2002-258] Family is the mainstay of Afghan society and families are often headed by a [patriarch](175409.md).[^cite_note-afghanistans.com-497] In the southern and eastern region, the people live according to the [Pashtun culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pashtun_culture) by following [Pashtunwali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pashtunwali) traditions.[^cite_note-498] Key tenets of Pashtunwali include [hospitality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Melmastia), the provision of [sanctuary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nanawatai) to those seeking refuge, and revenge for the shedding of blood.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDupree1997126-499] Tajiks and Hazaras, are culturally [Persian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persianization)[^cite_note-Kieffer-500] while the Uzbeks of Afghanistan follow [Turkic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkic_peoples) traditions. Some non-Pashtuns who live in proximity with Pashtuns have adopted Pashtunwali in a process called [Pashtunization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pashtunization), while some Pashtuns have been [Persianized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persianized). Those who have lived in Pakistan and Iran over the last 30 years have been further influenced by the cultures of those neighboring nations. The people of Afghanistan are known to be strongly religious.[^cite_note-The_Swedish_Committee_for_Afghanistan_(SCA)-465]

Pashtuns are noted for their tribal solidarity and high regard for personal honor.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarfield201259-501] There are various [Afghan tribes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_groups_in_Afghanistan), and an estimated 2–3 million [nomads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kochi_people).[^cite_note-502] Afghan culture is deeply [Islamic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_culture),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarfield201240–41-503] but pre-Islamic practices persist.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDupree1997104-504] [Child marriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Child_marriage_in_Afghanistan) is prevalent;[^cite_note-505] in 2017, [Human Rights Watch](46530.md) described the legal age for marriage of girls as being 16, or 15 with the consent of the father or a judge.[^cite_note-506] The most preferred marriage in Afghan society is to one's [parallel cousin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parallel_cousin), and the groom is often expected to pay a [bride price](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bride_price).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDupree1997122,_198-507]



A house occupied by nomadic *kochi* people in [Nangarhar Province](457263.md)

In the villages, families typically occupy [mudbrick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mudbrick) houses, or compounds with mudbrick or [stone walled](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stone_wall) houses. Villages typically have a headman (*malik*), a master for water distribution (*mirab*) and a religious teacher (*mullah*). Men would typically work on the fields, joined by women during harvest.[^cite_note-afghanistans.com-497] About 15% of the population are [nomadic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nomadic), locally called *[kochis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kochi_people)*.[^cite_note-Fisher-2002-258] When nomads pass villages they often buy supplies such as tea, wheat and [kerosene](16992.md) from the villagers; villagers buy [wool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wool) and milk from the nomads.[^cite_note-afghanistans.com-497]

[Afghan clothing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_clothing) for both men and women typically consists of various forms of [shalwar kameez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shalwar_kameez), especially *[perahan tunban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perahan_tunban)* and *[khet partug](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khet_partug)*. Women would normally wear a *[chador](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chador)* for head covering; some women, typically from highly conservative communities, wear the *[burqa](165826.md)*, a full body covering. These were worn by some women of the Pashtun community well before Islam came to the region, but the [Taliban](30635.md) enforced this dress on women when they were in power.[^cite_note-508] Another popular dress is the *[chapan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chapan)* which acts as a coat. The *[karakul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karakul_\(hat\))* is a hat made from the fur of a specific regional breed of sheep. It was favored by former kings of Afghanistan and became known to much of the world in the 21st century when it was constantly worn by President [Hamid Karzai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamid_Karzai).[^cite_note-509] The *[pakol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakol)* is another traditional hat originating from the far east of the country; it was popularly worn by the guerrilla leader [Ahmad Shah Massoud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmad_Shah_Massoud).[^cite_note-510] The *[Mazari hat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mazari_hat)* originates from northern Afghanistan.[^cite_note-511]

### Architecture

Main article: [Architecture of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture_of_Afghanistan)



Kabul skyline, displaying both historical and contemporary buildings

The nation has a complex history that has survived either in its current cultures or in the form of various languages and monuments. Afghanistan contains many remnants from all ages, including [Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greece) and [Buddhist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist) stupas, monasteries, monuments, temples, and Islamic minarets. Among the most well known are the [Great Mosque of Herat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Mosque_of_Herat), the [Blue Mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_Mosque_\(Mazar-i-Sharif\)), the [Minaret of Jam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minaret_of_Jam), the [Chil Zena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chil_Zena), the Qala-i Bost in [Lashkargah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lashkargah), the ancient Greek city of [Ai-Khanoum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ai-Khanoum).[^cite_note-512] However, many of its historic monuments have been damaged in modern times due to the civil wars.[^cite_note-513] The two famous [Buddhas of Bamiyan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhas_of_Bamiyan) were destroyed by the Taliban, who regarded them as [idolatrous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Idolatrous). As there was no colonialism in the modern era in Afghanistan, European-style architecture is rare but does exist: the Victory Arch at [Paghman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paghman) and the [Darul Aman Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Darul_Aman_Palace) in Kabul were built in this style in the 1920s. Afghan architecture also ranges [deep into India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Islamic_architecture) such as the city of [Agra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agra),[^cite_note-514] and the [tomb of Sher Shah Suri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tomb_of_Sher_Shah_Suri), an [Afghan Emperor of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sur_Empire).[^cite_note-515]

### Art and ceramics

Main article: [Afghan art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_art)



A traditional Afghan [embroidery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Embroidery) pattern

Carpet [weaving](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weaving) is an ancient practice in Afghanistan, and many of these are still [handmade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Handicraft) by tribal and nomadic people today.[^cite_note-aljazeera.com-443] Carpets have been produced in the region for thousands of years and traditionally done by women.[^cite_note-516] Some crafters express their feelings through the designs of rugs; for example after the outbreak of the [Soviet–Afghan War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet%E2%80%93Afghan_War), "[war rugs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_rugs)", a variant of [Afghan rugs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_rug), were created with designs representing pain and misery caused by the conflict.[^cite_note-517] Every province has its own specific characteristics in making rugs.[^cite_note-518] In some of the Turkic-populated areas in the north-west, bride and wedding ceremony prices are driven by the bride's weaving skills.[^cite_note-519]

[Pottery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pottery) has been crafted in Afghanistan for millennia. The village of [Istalif](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Istalif), north of Kabul, is in particular a major center, known for its unique turquoise and green pottery,[^cite_note-520] and their methods of crafting have remained the same for centuries.[^cite_note-521][^cite_note-522] Much of *[lapis lazuli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lapis_lazuli)* stones were earthed in modern-day Afghanistan which were used in [Chinese porcelain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_porcelain) as [cobalt blue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cobalt_blue), later used in ancient [Mesopotamia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mesopotamia) and Turkey.[^cite_note-523]

The lands of Afghanistan have a long history of art, with the world's earliest known usage of [oil painting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oil_painting) found in cave murals in the country.[^cite_note-524][^cite_note-525] A notable art style that developed in Afghanistan and eastern Pakistan is [Gandhara Art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gandhara_Art), produced by a fusion of [Greco-Roman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greco-Roman) art and [Buddhist art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_art) between the 1st and 7th centuries CE.[^cite_note-526] Later eras saw increased use of the [Persian miniature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_miniature) style, with [Kamaleddin Behzad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamaleddin_Behzad) of [Herat](14128.md) being one of the most notable miniature artists of the [Timurid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timurid_dynasty) and early [Safavid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Safavid) periods. Since the 1900s, the nation began to use Western techniques in art. [Abdul Ghafoor Breshna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdul_Ghafoor_Breshna) was a prominent Afghan painter and sketch artist from Kabul during the 20th century.

### Literature

Main article: [Afghan literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_literature)

Classic [Persian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_literature) and [Pashto poetry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pashto_poetry) are a cherished part of Afghan culture. Poetry has always been one of the major educational pillars in the region, to the level that it has integrated itself into culture.[^cite_note-527] One of the poetic styles is called [landay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landay_\(poetry\)). A popular theme in Afghan folklore and mythology are [Divs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dev_\(mythology\)), monstrous creatures.[^cite_note-528] Thursdays are traditionally "poetry night" in the city of [Herat](14128.md) when men, women and children gather and recite both ancient and modern poems.[^cite_note-529]

Three mystical authors are considered true national glories (although claimed with equal ardor by Iran), namely: [Khwaja Abdullah Ansari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khwaja_Abdullah_Ansari) of Herat, a great mystic and [Sufi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sufi) saint in the 11th century, [Sanai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanai) of [Ghazni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghazni), author of mystical poems in the 12th century, and, finally, [Rumi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rumi) of [Balkh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balkh), in the 13th century, considered the greatest mystical poet of the Muslim world. The Afghan Pashto literature, although quantitatively remarkable and in great growth in the last century, has always had an essentially local meaning and importance, feeling the influence of both Persian literature and the contiguous literatures of India. Both main literatures, from the second half of the nineteenth century, have shown themselves to be sensitive to genres, movements and stylistic features imported from Europe.

[Khushal Khan Khattak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khushal_Khan_Khattak) of the 17th century is considered the national poet. Other notable poets include [Rabi'a Balkhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rabi'a_Balkhi), [Jami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jami), [Rahman Baba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rahman_Baba), [Khalilullah Khalili](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khalilullah_Khalili), and [Parween Pazhwak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parween_Pazhwak).[^cite_note-530]

### Music

Main article: [Music of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Afghanistan)



The Afghan [rubab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rubab_\(instrument\))

Afghan classical music has close historical links with [Indian classical music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_classical_music) and use the same Hindustani terminology and theories like [raga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raga). Genres of this style of music include [ghazal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghazal) (poetic music) and instruments such as the Indian [tabla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tabla), [sitar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sitar) and [harmonium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harmonium), and local instruments like [zerbaghali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zerbaghali), as well as [dayereh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dayereh) and [tanbur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tanbur) which are also known in Central Asia, the Caucasus and the Middle East. The [rubab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rubab_\(instrument\)) is the country's national instrument and precurses the Indian [sarod](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarod) instrument. Some of the famous artists of classical music include [Ustad Sarahang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ustad_Sarahang) and [Sarban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdul_Rahim_Sarban).[^cite_note-Songlines-531]

Pop music developed in the 1950s through [Radio Kabul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radio_Kabul) and was influential in social change. During this time female artists also started appearing, at first [Mermon Parwin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mermon_Parwin).[^cite_note-Songlines-531] Perhaps the most famous artist of this genre was [Ahmad Zahir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmad_Zahir), who synthesized many genres and continues to be renowned for his voice and rich lyrics long after his death in 1979.[^cite_note-532][^cite_note-Songlines-531] Other notable masters of traditional or popular Afghan music include [Nashenas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nashenas), [Ubaidullah Jan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ubaidullah_Jan), [Mahwash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahwash), [Ahmad Wali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmad_Wali), [Farhad Darya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Farhad_Darya), and [Naghma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naghma_\(singer\)).[^cite_note-533]

[Attan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attan) is the national dance of Afghanistan, a group dance popularly performed by Afghans of all backgrounds.[^cite_note-534] The dance is considered part of Afghan identity.[^cite_note-535]

### Media and entertainment

Main article: [Mass media in Afghanistan](16568985.md)

Afghanistan has around 350 [radio stations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_radio_stations_in_Afghanistan) and over 200 television stations.[^cite_note-TOLO-2019-536] [Radio Television Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radio_Television_Afghanistan), originating from 1925, is the state public broadcaster. Television programs began airing in the 1970s and today there are many private television channels such as [TOLO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TOLO_\(TV_channel\)) and [Shamshad TV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shamshad_TV). The first Afghan newspaper was published in 1873,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDupree1997405-537] and there are hundreds of print outlets today.[^cite_note-TOLO-2019-536] By the 1920s, [Radio Kabul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radio_Kabul) was broadcasting local radio services.[^cite_note-538] [Voice of America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voice_of_America), [BBC](37627.md), and [Radio Free Europe/Radio Liberty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radio_Free_Europe/Radio_Liberty) (RFE/RL) broadcast in both of Afghanistan's official languages on radio.[^cite_note-539] Press restrictions have been gradually relaxed and private media diversified since 2002, after more than two decades of tight controls.

Afghans have long been accustomed to watching Indian [Bollywood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bollywood) films and listening to its [filmi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filmi) songs.[^cite_note-540] It has been claimed that Afghanistan is among the biggest markets for the Hindi film industry.[^cite_note-541] The stereotypes of [Afghans in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghans_in_India) (*Kabuliwala* or *Pathani*) have also been represented in some Bollywood films by actors.[^cite_note-542] Many Bollywood film stars have roots in Afghanistan, including [Salman Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salman_Khan), [Saif Ali Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saif_Ali_Khan), [Aamir Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aamir_Khan), [Feroz Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feroz_Khan_\(Indian_actor\)), [Kader Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kader_Khan), [Naseeruddin Shah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naseeruddin_Shah), [Zarine Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zarine_Khan), [Celina Jaitly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Celina_Jaitly), and a number of others. Several Bollywood films have been shot inside Afghanistan, including *[Dharmatma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dharmatma)*, *[Khuda Gawah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khuda_Gawah)*, *[Escape from Taliban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Escape_from_Taliban)*, and *[Kabul Express](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kabul_Express)*.

### Cuisine

Main article: [Afghan cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_cuisine)



[Non](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naan), the most widely consumed bread in Afghanistan

Afghan cuisine is largely based upon the nation's chief crops, such as wheat, maize, [barley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barley) and rice. Accompanying these staples are native fruits and vegetables as well as dairy products such as milk, [yogurt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yogurt), and [whey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whey). [Kabuli palaw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kabuli_palaw) is the [national dish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_dish) of Afghanistan.[^cite_note-543] The nation's culinary specialties reflect its ethnic and geographic diversity.[^cite_note-544] Afghanistan is known for its high-quality [pomegranates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pomegranate_production_in_Afghanistan), grapes, and sweet melons.[^cite_note-545] Tea is a favorite drink among Afghans. A typical Afghan diet consists of [naan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naan), yogurt, rice, and meat.[^cite_note-afghanistans.com-497]

### Holidays and festivals



*Haft mewa* (seven-fruit syrup), popularly consumed during Nowruz

Afghanistan's official New Year starts with [Nowruz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nowruz) (natively known as "*Nawroz*"), an ancient tradition that started as a [Zoroastrian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zoroastrian) celebration in present-day Iran, and with which it shares the annual celebration along with several other countries. It occurs every year at the [vernal equinox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/March_equinox). [In Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nauruz_in_Afghanistan), Nowruz is typically celebrated with music and dance, as well as holding [buzkashi](169471.md) tournaments.[^cite_note-546]

[Yaldā](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yald%C4%81), another nationally celebrated ancient tradition,[^cite_note-547] commemorates the ancient goddess [Mithra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mithra) and marks the longest night of the year on the eve of the [winter solstice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Winter_solstice) (*Čilli-yi Zimistān*; usually falling on 20 or 21 December),[^cite_note-548][^cite_note-549] during which families gather together to recite poetry and eat fruit.[^cite_note-550][^cite_note-551]

As a predominantly Muslim country, Islamic events and festivals such as [Ramadan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramadan), [Eid al-Fitr](154944.md) and [Ashura](488563.md) are widely celebrated annually in Afghanistan. The Sikh festival of [Vaisakhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vaisakhi) is celebrated by the Sikh community[^cite_note-552] and the Hindu festival [Diwali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diwali) by the Hindu community.[^cite_note-553]

[National Independence Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_Independence_Day) is celebrated on 19 August to mark the [Anglo-Afghan Treaty of 1919](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-Afghan_Treaty_of_1919) and the country's full independence.[^cite_note-Factbook-261] Several international celebrations are also officially held in Afghanistan, such as [International Workers' Day](39353050.md),[^cite_note-554] and [International Women's Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Women's_Day).[^cite_note-555] Some regional festivals include the [Red Flower Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nowruz#Afghanistan) (during Nowruz) in [Mazar-i-Sharif](20517.md),[^cite_note-556] and the Damboora Festival in [Bamyan Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bamyan_Province).[^cite_note-557]

### Sports

Main article: [Sport in Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sport_in_Afghanistan)

See also: [Traditional games of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Traditional_games_of_Afghanistan)



The ancient national sport of Afghanistan, [Buzkashi](169471.md)

Sport in Afghanistan is managed by the Afghan Sports Federation. [Cricket](25675557.md) and association football are the two most popular sports in the country.[^cite_note-558][^cite_note-559] The Afghan Sports Federation promotes cricket, association football, basketball, volleyball, golf, [handball](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Team_handball), [boxing](4243.md), [taekwondo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taekwondo), [weightlifting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olympic_weightlifting), [bodybuilding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bodybuilding), track and field, [skating](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ice_skating), [bowling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bowling), [snooker](27378.md), [chess](5134.md), and other sports.

The [Afghanistan national basketball team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghanistan_national_basketball_team) won the first team sports title at the [2010 South Asian Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_South_Asian_Games).[^cite_note-560] In 2012, the country's [3x3 basketball team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghanistan_national_3x3_team) won the gold medal at the [2012 Asian Beach Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/3-on-3_basketball_at_the_2012_Asian_Beach_Games). In 2013, Afghanistan's [football team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghanistan_national_football_team) followed as it won the [SAFF Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SAFF_Championship).[^cite_note-SAFF2013-561]

The [Afghan national cricket team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_national_cricket_team), which was formed in 2001, won the [2009–10 ICC Intercontinental Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2009%E2%80%9310_ICC_Intercontinental_Cup).[^cite_note-562] It won the [ACC Twenty20 Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ACC_Twenty20_Cup) in 2007, 2009, 2011 and 2013. The team played in the [2015](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015_Cricket_World_Cup), [2019](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Cricket_World_Cup), and [2023 Cricket World Cups](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Cricket_World_Cup).[^cite_note-563] The [Afghanistan Cricket Board](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghanistan_Cricket_Board) (ACB) is the official governing body of the sport and is based in Kabul. The [Alokozay Kabul International Cricket Ground](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alokozay_Kabul_International_Cricket_Ground) serves as the nation's main cricket stadium. There are several other stadiums throughout the country, including the [Ghazi Amanullah Khan International Cricket Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghazi_Amanullah_Khan_International_Cricket_Stadium) near [Jalalabad](16274.md). Domestically, cricket is played between teams from different provinces.

The [Afghanistan national football team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghanistan_national_football_team) has been competing in international [football](10568.md) since 1941.[^cite_note-564] The national team plays its home games at the [Ghazi Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghazi_Stadium) in Kabul, while [football in Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Football_in_Afghanistan) is governed by the [Afghanistan Football Federation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghanistan_Football_Federation). The national team has never competed or qualified for the [FIFA World Cup](11370.md) but won an international football trophy in 2013.[^cite_note-SAFF2013-561] The country also has a national team in the sport of futsal, a 5-a-side variation of football.

The traditional and the national sport of Afghanistan is [buzkashi](169471.md), particularly popular in the north.[^cite_note-565] It is similar to [polo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polo), played by horsemen in two teams, each trying to grab and hold a goat carcass.[^cite_note-566] The [Afghan Hound](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_Hound) (a type of running dog) originated in Afghanistan and was used in [wolf hunting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wolf_hunting_with_dogs).[^cite_note-567]

"The Taliban Takeover in Afghanistan and Security Paradox". *[Journal of Asian Security and International Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Journal_of_Asian_Security_and_International_Affairs)*. **9** (3): 383–401. Afghanistan is now controlled by a militant group that operates out of a totalitarian ideology. Madadi, Sayed (6 September 2022). ["Dysfunctional centralization and growing fragility under Taliban rule"](https://www.mei.edu/publications/dysfunctional-centralization-and-growing-fragility-under-taliban-rule). *[Middle East Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_East_Institute)*.
